But after this period of time, the overthrow of the rule of Astyages, son of Cyaxares, by Cyrus, son of Cambyses, and the growing greatness of the Persians, caused Croesus to leave off his mourning and turned his attention to cutting short the power of the Persians, if by any means he might, while it was still growing and before they should have become great.

The last king of the Medes, who dreamed twice about his daughter, ordered his grandson killed, served Harpagos his own son at dinner, and lost his kingdom to the grandson who survived.

## modern-en · harpagos · 79:0

So when the armies had met in that plain in front of the city of Sardis—a plain wide and open, through which flow rivers (and especially the river Hyllus), all rushing down to join the largest, called the Hermus, which flows from the mountain sacred to the Mother surnamed “of Dindymus” and runs out into the sea by the city of Phocaea—then Cyrus, when he saw the Lydians being arrayed for battle, fearing their horsemen, did as Harpagus the Mede suggested. He gathered all the camels in the train of his army that were carrying provisions and baggage, took off their burdens, and set men upon them equipped like cavalry. Having thus furnished them, he ordered them to go in front of the rest of the army toward the horsemen of Croesus; and after the camel-troop he commanded the infantry to follow; and behind the infantry he placed his whole force of cavalry. When all his men had been placed in their several positions, he charged them to spare none of the rest of the Lydians, slaying all who came in their way, but they were not to kill Croesus himself, not even if he should resist when he was captured. Such was his charge. He had set the camels opposite the horsemen for this reason: because the horse is afraid of the camel and cannot endure either to see its shape or to scent its smell. For this reason, then, the trick had been devised, in order that the cavalry of Croesus might be useless—the very force with which the Lydian king expected most to shine. And as they were coming together to battle, as soon as the horses scented the camels and saw them, they turned back, and the hopes of Croesus were at once brought to nothing. The Lydians, however, did not behave as cowards on that account, but when they perceived what was happening, they leapt from their horses and fought the Persians on foot. At length, however, when many had fallen on either side, the Lydians turned to flight; and having been driven within the wall of their fortress, they were besieged by the Persians.

Astyages’s kinsman, ordered to expose the infant Cyrus and unable to do it. Astyages fed him his own son for it; Harpagos waited years and then handed Astyages’s army to Cyrus. He afterwards conquered Ionia for Persia. The two editions spell him Harpagos and Harpagus.

## modern-en · phraortes · 72:0

Now for the following reasons Croesus was marching into Cappadocia: first, because he wished to add its land to his own possessions, and then especially because he had confidence in the oracle and wished to take vengeance on Cyrus on behalf of Astyages. For Cyrus, son of Cambyses, had conquered Astyages and was keeping him in captivity; Astyages, who was king of the Medes, was Croesus’s brother-in-law, and he had become Croesus’s brother-in-law in this way. A horde of nomad Scythians, at feud with the rest of their people, withdrew and sought refuge in the land of the Medes. At this time the ruler of the Medes was Cyaxares, son of Phraortes, son of Deioces, who at first dealt kindly with these Scythians, as they were suppliants for his protection. Esteeming them highly, he handed over boys to them to learn their speech and the art of shooting with the bow. Time went by, and the Scythians used to go out continually to hunt and always brought back something, until on one occasion they took nothing, and when they returned with empty hands Cyaxares—who, as he showed on this occasion, was not of an eminently good disposition—treated them very harshly and insulted them. The Scythians, having received such treatment from Cyaxares, considered that they had suffered indignity, and planned to kill and cut up one of the boys who were being instructed among them, and, dressing his flesh as they had been accustomed to dress the flesh of wild animals, to carry it to Cyaxares and give it to him, pretending that it was game taken in hunting. Once they had done so, their plan was to make their way as quickly as possible to Alyattes, son of Sadyattes, at Sardis. This was accordingly done, and Cyaxares together with the guests who ate at his table tasted of that meat. The Scythians, having done this, became suppliants for the protection of Alyattes.

Deioces’s son, who subdued the Persians and died attacking the Assyrians of Nineveh.

## modern-en · mandane · 106:0

After this Cyaxares died, having reigned forty years, including those years during which the Scythians had ruled, and Astyages, son of Cyaxares, received the kingdom from him. To him was born a daughter whom he named Mandane; and in his sleep it seemed to him that there passed from her so much water as to fill his city and even to flood the whole of Asia. This dream he handed over to the Magian interpreters of dreams, and when he heard from them the truth of each detail, he became afraid. And afterwards, when this Mandane was of an age to have a husband, he did not give her in marriage to any of the Medes who were his peers, because he feared the vision; but he gave her to a Persian named Cambyses, whom he found to be of good descent and of a quiet disposition, counting him in station much below a Mede of middle rank.

Astyages’s daughter and Cyrus’s mother, married off to a quiet Persian because of what her father dreamed about her.

## modern-en · mitradates · 109:0

He immediately sent a messenger to one of Astyages' herdsmen who, he knew, grazed his cattle on the most suitable pastures in mountains full of wild beasts. The herdsman's name was Mitradates, and he lived with a woman who was also a slave of Astyages. Her Greek name would be Kyno (meaning "dog"), since her Median name, Spako, translates to the same thing—the Median word for dog being "spaka." The foothills where this herdsman kept his cattle lie north of Agbatana, toward the Euxine Sea. That part of Media, bordering Saspeiran territory, is very mountainous, heavily forested, and high, while the rest of Media is entirely flat. When the herdsman came with great speed at the summons, Harpagos said: "Astyages commands you to take this child and leave it in the wildest part of the mountains, so that it dies as quickly as possible. He told me to say this: if you do not kill the child and try to save it by any means, you will die by the most terrible death. I have been ordered to verify that it is exposed."

The herdsman ordered to expose the infant Cyrus, whose own newborn son had just died; his wife asked for the living child instead.

## modern-en · artembares-mede · 113:0

When the boy was ten years old, something happened to him which made his true identity known. He was playing in the village where the stalls for oxen stood, playing in the road with other boys of his age. And the boys in their game chose as their king this one who was called the son of the herdsman. He assigned some of them to build palaces, others to be spearmen of his guard, one to be the eye of the king, and one to bear messages — appointing a duty to each one. Now one of these boys who was playing with the rest, the son of Artembares, a man of repute among the Medes, did not do what Cyrus had appointed him to do. So Cyrus ordered the other boys to seize him by hand and foot, and when they obeyed his command he dealt with the boy very roughly, scourging him. As soon as he was let go, being all the more angry because he considered he had been treated with indignity, he went down to the city and complained to his father of the treatment he had received from Cyrus — calling him not Cyrus, for this was not yet his name, but the son of the herdsman of Astyages. And Artembares, in the anger of the moment, went at once to Astyages, taking the boy with him, and declared that he had suffered treatment that was unfitting, saying: “O king, by your slave, the son of a herdsman, we have been thus outraged” — and he showed him the shoulders of his son.

The Mede whose son was whipped by the boy Cyrus playing at being king — the complaint that made Astyages look at the boy’s face.

## modern-en · mazares · 155:0

Croesus proposed this because he thought it was better for the Lydians than being sold into slavery. He knew that without a strong argument, he could not change Cyrus' mind. He also feared that even if the Lydians escaped this time, they might revolt again and be destroyed. Cyrus was pleased with the advice. He calmed his anger and said he would follow Croesus' suggestion. He summoned a Mede named Mazares and ordered him to tell the Lydians exactly what Croesus had recommended. He also ordered Mazares to sell into slavery everyone who had joined the Lydians in besieging Sardis—and above all, to bring Pactyas back alive.

The Mede sent to put down Pactyas and reduce the Ionians; he died in the middle of it.

## modern-en · tabalos · 152:0

When the herald had spoken, Cyrus reportedly asked the Greeks in his company who the Spartans were and how many. On hearing the answer, he told the Spartan herald: "I have never yet feared the kind of men who have a special place in the center of their city where they gather to cheat each other with oaths." This was a jab at the Greeks for having marketplaces, something the Persians do not use—they never buy or sell in markets. He added: "If I stay healthy, it will be their own misfortunes they discuss, not those of the Ionians." Cyrus aimed this threat at all the Greeks, since they had marketplaces. He then appointed a Persian named Tabalos as governor of Sardis and gave a Lydian named Pactyas the task of transporting Croesus' gold. Cyrus himself marched back toward Agbatana, taking Croesus with him. He considered the Ionians too unimportant to deal with personally. Babylon, the Bactrians, the Sacae, and the Egyptians—those were his primary concerns. He intended to lead campaigns against them himself and send a subordinate to handle the Ionians.

The Persian left as governor of Sardis, besieged in the acropolis by Pactyas.

## modern-en · darius · 129:0

So Astyages was deposed after ruling thirty-five years. The Medes bowed under Persian rule because of his cruelty, having themselves ruled Asia above the river Halys for one hundred and twenty-eight years, not counting the Scythian period. Later they would rebel against the Persians and regain independence, but they were defeated again by Darius' forces. But at this point in time, the Persians under Cyrus rose against the Medes and from then on ruled Asia. As for Astyages, Cyrus kept him at his court and did him no further harm for the rest of his life. That is how Cyrus was born, raised, and became king. And afterward, as I have already described, he conquered Croesus, who had attacked him first. With that victory, he became master of all Asia.

Darius son of Hystaspes, later king of Persia, who appears in Book 1 only as the young man in Cyrus’s dream and as the future plunderer of a Babylonian statue. The two editions spell him Darius and Dareios.

## modern-en · hystaspes · 182:0

There is moreover in the temple at Babylon another cell below, in which is a great image of Zeus sitting, made of gold, and beside it is placed a large table of gold, and his footstool and seat are of gold also; and, as the Chaldeans reported, the weight of the gold of which these things are made is eight hundred talents. Outside this cell is an altar of gold, and there is also another altar of great size, where full-grown animals are sacrificed, whereas on the golden altar it is not lawful to sacrifice any but young sucklings. On the larger altar the Chaldeans offer one thousand talents of frankincense every year at the time when they celebrate the feast in honor of this god. There was moreover in these precincts, still remaining at the time of Cyrus, a statue twelve cubits high, of solid gold. This I did not myself see, but what is related by the Chaldeans, I relate. Against this statue Dareios the son of Hystaspes formed a design, but he did not venture to take it; it was taken, however, by Xerxes the son of Dareios, who also killed the priest when he forbade him to meddle with the statue. This temple, then, is thus adorned with magnificence, and there are also many private votive-offerings.

Darius’s father, a Persian in Cyrus’s army whom Cyrus sent home to watch his son after the dream.

## modern-en · tomyris · 204:0

Now the ruler of the Massagetai was a woman, who was queen after the death of her husband, and her name was Tomyris. To her Cyrus sent and wooed her, pretending that he desired to have her for his wife. But Tomyris, understanding that he was wooing not herself but rather the kingdom of the Massagetai, rejected his approaches. Cyrus after this, as he made no progress by craft, marched to the Araxes, and proceeded to make an expedition openly against the Massagetai, forming bridges of boats over the river for his army to cross, and building towers upon the vessels which gave them passage across the river.

Queen of the Massagetai, who refused Cyrus’s offer of marriage, warned him to keep to his own country, lost her son to his trick with the wine, and put his head in a skin of blood so that he could drink his fill.

## modern-en · spargapises · 210:0

Hystaspes, then, having made answer with these words and having crossed over the Araxes, was going his way to the Persian land to keep watch over his son Dareios for Cyrus; and Cyrus meanwhile went forward and made a march of one day from the Araxes according to the suggestion of Croesus. After this, when Cyrus and the best part of the army of the Persians had marched back to the Araxes, and those who were unfit for fighting had been left behind, then a third part of the army of the Massagetai came to the attack and proceeded to slay — not without resistance — those who had been left behind of the army of Cyrus. Seeing the feast that was set forth, when they had overcome their enemies, they lay down and feasted; and being satiated with food and wine, they went to sleep. Tomyris’s son, taken by Cyrus’s stratagem with the feast and the unmixed wine, who asked to be unbound and killed himself.

## modern-en · labynetus · 73:0

After this, since Alyattes would not give up the Scythians when Cyaxares demanded them, war arose between the Lydians and the Medes that lasted five years. In those years the Medes often defeated the Lydians and the Lydians often defeated the Medes, and they fought, among other engagements, a battle by night. And as they still carried on the war with equally balanced fortune, in the sixth year a battle took place in which, when the fight had begun, it happened that day suddenly turned to night. This change of day Thales of Miletus had foretold to the Ionians, setting as its limit this very year in which the change took place. The Lydians and Medes, when they saw that night had come in place of day, ceased fighting and both became much more eager that peace should be made between them. Those who brought about the peace between them were Syennesis the Cilician and Labynetus the Babylonian. These were the men who urged them to take the oaths, and they also arranged an exchange of marriages: they determined that Alyattes should give his daughter Aryenis to Astyages, son of Cyaxares, since without the compulsion of a strong tie agreements tend not to hold firmly together. Now these nations observe the same ceremonies in the taking of oaths as the Greeks do, and in addition they make an incision in the skin of their arms and then lick up the blood, each from the other.

The king of Babylon, named twice — as one of the two mediators of the peace between Lydia and Media, and as the ruler Cyrus’s canals were dug against.

## modern-en · chaldeans · 180:0

This wall, then, which I have mentioned, is as it were a breastplate for the town, and another wall runs round within it, not much weaker for defense than the first, but enclosing a smaller space. In each division of the city there was a building in the midst: in the one, the king's palace of great extent and strongly fortified round; in the other, the temple of Zeus Belos with bronze gates, and this exists still up to my time, and measures two furlongs each way, being of a square shape. In the midst of the temple is built a solid tower, measuring a furlong both in length and breadth; and on this tower another tower has been erected, and another again upon this, and so on up to the number of eight towers. An ascent to these has been built running outside round about all the towers; and when one reaches about the middle of the ascent, one finds a stopping-place and seats to rest upon, on which those who ascend sit down and rest. On the top of the last tower there is a large cell, and in the cell a large couch is laid, well covered, and beside it a golden table is placed; and there is no image there set up, nor does any human being spend the night there, except only one woman of the natives of the place, whomsoever the god shall choose from all the women, as the Chaldeans — who are the priests of this god — say.

The priests of Bel at Babylon, who told Herodotus what stood in the temple and what had been taken from it.

## modern-en · solon · 29:0

when these, I say, had been subdued and while he was still extending his Lydian dominion, there came to Sardis — which was then at the height of its wealth — all the wise men of Hellas who happened to be alive at the time, each brought there for his own reason. Among them was Solon the Athenian, who, after making laws for the Athenians at their request, had left his native country for ten years and sailed away, saying that he wanted to see various lands, so that he would not be forced to repeal any of the laws he had proposed. For the Athenians on their own could not do so, since they had bound themselves with solemn oaths to live for ten years under the laws Solon had made for them.

The Athenian lawgiver, who left Athens for ten years so that his laws could not be unmade, came to Sardis, and refused to call Croesus happy while he was still alive.

## modern-en · tellus · 30:0

So Solon, having left his native country for that reason and for the sake of seeing the world, came to Amasis in Egypt and also to Croesus at Sardis. There he was entertained as a guest by Croesus in the king's palace; and afterwards, on the third or fourth day, at Croesus's order, the servants led Solon around to see the royal treasuries, and they showed him everything — how great and magnificent it all was. After Solon had looked at it all and examined it at leisure, Croesus asked him, “My Athenian guest, much report of you has come to us, both of your wisdom and of your travels — how, in search of wisdom, you have visited many lands to see them. Now, then, a desire has come upon me to ask whether you have seen any man whom you consider the happiest of all men.” He asked this supposing that he himself was the happiest, but Solon, using no flattery and speaking only the truth, said, “Yes, O king — Tellus the Athenian.” Croesus, astonished at this, asked him earnestly, “In what way do you judge Tellus to be the happiest?” Solon replied, “Tellus, in the first place, lived when his native state was prospering, and he had sons who were fine and good, and saw children born to each of them and all of them growing up. And secondly, he had what with us passes for wealth, and he met with the most glorious end of his life: for when the Athenians fought a battle against their neighbors at Eleusis, he came up in support, routed the enemy, and there died a most noble death. The Athenians buried him publicly on the spot where he fell and honored him greatly.”

The Athenian Solon names as the happiest of men: he lived well, saw his children’s children, and died fighting at Eleusis and was buried where he fell.

## modern-en · cleobis · 31:0

After Solon had moved Croesus to ask further with his story about Tellus—recounting how many marks of happiness the man possessed—the king asked again whom he had judged worthy to be placed next after him, supposing that he himself would certainly win at least the second place. But Solon replied: “Cleobis and Biton. These men, Argives by birth, had wealth enough to live on and, in addition, bodily strength such as I shall describe. Both had equally won prizes in the games, and the following tale is also told of them. There was a festival of Hera among the Argives, and it was absolutely necessary that their mother should be carried in a cart to the temple. But the oxen were not brought up from the field in time. Barred by the shortage of time from any other solution, the young men put themselves under the yoke and drew the wagon, their mother riding upon it. So they pulled it for forty-five stades and arrived at the temple. After they had accomplished this and had been seen by the gathered crowd, a most excellent ending was given to their lives, and in this matter the deity showed that it is better for a man to die than to continue living. For the Argive men stood around praising the strength of the young men, and the Argive women praised the mother for having been lucky enough to bear such sons. The mother, overjoyed both by the deed itself and by the reports of it, took her stand before the image of the goddess and prayed that she would grant to her sons Cleobis and Biton, who had so greatly honored her, the gift that is best for a man to receive. After this prayer, once the young men had sacrificed and feasted, they lay down to sleep inside the temple itself and never rose again, but were held fast in that final ending. The Argives had statues made in their likeness and dedicated them as offerings at Delphi, believing that they had proved themselves most excellent men.”

One of two Argive brothers who pulled their mother’s ox-cart to the temple of Hera; she prayed for the best thing a man can have, and they died in their sleep.

## modern-en · peisistratus · 58:0

Of these two peoples, then, Croesus was informed that the Athenian was held in subjection and torn by faction under Peisistratus, son of Hippocrates, who was at that time tyrant of the Athenians. For to Hippocrates, while he was still a private citizen and had gone to view the Olympic games, a great marvel had occurred. After he had offered the sacrifice, the cauldrons standing upon the hearth, full of pieces of flesh and of water, boiled over without fire beneath them and ran over. Chilon the Lacedaemonian, who happened to be present and to have seen the marvel, advised Hippocrates first not to bring into his house a wife to bear him children, and secondly, if he already had one, to dismiss her, and, if he happened to have a son, to disown him. But Hippocrates, they say, was not willing to be persuaded by Chilon’s advice. And so afterward this Peisistratus was born to him. When the Athenians of the shore were at feud with those of the plain—Megacles, son of Alcmaeon, being leader of the first faction, and Lycurgus, son of Aristolaidas, leader of that of the plain—Peisistratus aimed at the tyranny for himself and gathered a third party. After he had collected supporters and proclaimed himself leader of the men of the mountain-lands, he contrived the following device: he inflicted wounds upon himself and upon his mules, and then drove his chariot into the market-place as if he had just escaped from his enemies, who, he alleged, had tried to kill him as he was driving into the country. He then begged of the commons that he might obtain some protection from them, for before this he had gained a reputation in his command against the Megarians, during which he took Nisaea and performed other signal services. The commons of the Athenians, being deceived, gave him a body of men chosen from among the dwellers in the city—men who became not the spearmen of Peisistratus but his club-men, for they followed behind him bearing wooden clubs. These rose up with Peisistratus and seized the Acropolis. Then Peisistratus became ruler of the Athenians, without disturbing the existing magistrates or changing the ancient laws; but he administered the State under that constitution which was already established, ordering it fairly and well.

Tyrant of Athens three times over: once by wounding himself and getting a bodyguard, once with a tall woman dressed as Athena riding into the city beside him, and once by force and mercenaries.

## modern-en · lygdamis · 60:0

Having thus recovered the tyranny in the manner described, Peisistratus, in accordance with the agreement he had made with Megacles, married the daughter of Megacles. But as he already had young sons, and as the descendants of Alcmaeon were said to be under a curse, he did not wish children to be born to him from his newly married wife, and so had intercourse with her in an unaccustomed manner. At first the woman kept this secret, but afterwards she told her mother—whether in answer to her inquiry or not I cannot say—and the mother told her husband Megacles. He then was deeply indignant at being dishonored by Peisistratus, and in his anger immediately moved to compose his quarrel with the men of his faction. When Peisistratus heard what was being done against him, he departed entirely from the land and came to Eretria, where he took counsel together with his sons. When the advice of Hippias prevailed—that they should try to win back the tyranny—they began to gather gifts of money from those states that owed them obligations for favors received. Many contributed great sums, but the Thebans surpassed the rest in the giving of money. Then, not to make the story long, time passed and at last everything was prepared for their return. For certain Argives came as mercenaries from the Peloponnesus, and a man of Naxos called Lygdamis had come to them of his own accord, showing very great zeal in providing both money and men.

The Naxian who gave Peisistratus money and men for his third return, and was set up as tyrant of Naxos for it.

## modern-en · lycurgus-spartan · 64:0

Such was the condition of things that Croesus heard was prevailing among the Athenians during this time. As for the Lacedaemonians, he heard that they had escaped from great troubles and had now got the better of the Tegeans in the war. For while Leon and Hegesicles were kings of Sparta, the Lacedaemonians, who had been successful in all their other wars, suffered disaster only in the one they waged against the men of Tegea. Moreover, in earlier times they had had almost the worst laws of any Greeks, both in matters that concerned themselves alone and also in that they had no dealings with strangers. And they made their change to a good constitution of laws in this way: Lycurgus, a man of the Spartans who was held in high repute, came to the Oracle at Delphi, and as he entered the sanctuary of the temple the Pythian prophetess at once spoke as follows:

The lawgiver who gave Sparta its whole constitution — brought from Crete, or given by the Pythia, depending on who is telling it — and turned the worst-governed Greeks into the best.

Such was the condition of things that Croesus heard was prevailing among the Athenians during this time. As for the Lacedaemonians, he heard that they had escaped from great troubles and had now got the better of the Tegeans in the war. For while Leon and Hegesicles were kings of Sparta, the Lacedaemonians, who had been successful in all their other wars, suffered disaster only in the one they waged against the men of Tegea. Moreover, in earlier times they had had almost the worst laws of any Greeks, both in matters that concerned themselves alone and also in that they had no dealings with strangers. And they made their change to a good constitution of laws in this way: Lycurgus, a man of the Spartans who was held in high repute, came to the Oracle at Delphi, and as he entered the sanctuary of the temple the Pythian prophetess at once spoke as follows:

A king of Sparta in the generation before Anaxandrides, under whom the Spartans beat everyone except Tegea.

## modern-en · anaxandrides · 66:0

In the earlier war, then, as I said, they struggled against the Tegeans continually with ill success; but in the time of Croesus, and in the reigns of Anaxandrides and Ariston at Lacedaemon, the Spartans had at last become victors in the war. And they became victors in the following manner: since they kept being defeated by the men of Tegea, they sent messengers to consult the Oracle at Delphi and asked what god they should propitiate in order to prevail over the men of Tegea in the war. The Pythian prophetess answered them that they should bring into their land the bones of Orestes, son of Agamemnon. But as they were not able to find the grave of Orestes, they sent men again to go to the god and ask about the place where Orestes lay. When the messengers who were sent asked this, the prophetess said as follows:

King of Sparta at the time of Croesus’s embassy.

## modern-en · othryades · 81:0

So in sending to his other allies, he sent also to Lacedaemon. But these—the Spartans, I mean—had at this very time (as it happened) a quarrel of their own in hand with the Argives about the district called Thyrea. For the Lacedaemonians had cut off this Thyrea, which was part of the Argive territory, and had taken it for themselves. At that time the Argives possessed the whole region toward the west extending as far down as Malea, both the parts on the mainland and also the island of Cythera with the other islands. When the Argives had come to the rescue to save their territory from being cut off, the two sides came to a parley and agreed that three hundred men should fight on each side, and whichever side had the better in the fight, that nation should possess the disputed land. They also agreed that the main body of each army should withdraw to its own country and not stand by while the contest was fought, for fear that, if the armies were present, one side, seeing their countrymen suffering defeat, would come up to their support. Having made this agreement, they withdrew, and chosen men of both sides were left behind and engaged in combat with one another. They fought and proved themselves evenly matched, and at last, of six hundred men, only three were left alive—on the Argive side Alcenor and Chromius, and on the Lacedaemonian side Othryades. These were left alive when night came on. The two Argives, supposing that they were the victors, set off to run back to Argos; but the Lacedaemonian Othryades, after stripping the corpses of the Argives and carrying their arms to his own camp, remained in his place. On the next day both sides came to the spot to inquire about the result, and for some time both claimed the victory for themselves: the one side saying that more of them had survived, the others declaring that those men had fled whereas their own man had stood his ground and had stripped the corpses of the other party. At length, because of this dispute, they fell upon one another and began to fight again, and after many had fallen on both sides, the Lacedaemonians were victorious. The Argives then cut their hair short, whereas formerly they were required by law to wear it long, and they made a law with a curse attached: that from that time forth no Argive man should grow his hair long nor their women wear ornaments of gold until they had won back Thyrea. The Lacedaemonians, on the contrary, laid down for themselves the opposite law: that they should wear long hair from that time forward, whereas before that time they had not worn it long. And they say that the one man left alive of the three hundred, Othryades, being ashamed to return to Sparta when all his comrades had been slain, killed himself there in Thyrea.

The one Spartan left standing after the Battle of the Champions at Thyrea, who stripped the Argive dead and stayed on the field — and afterwards killed himself for shame at outliving his three hundred.

## modern-en · chilon · 58:0

Of these two peoples, then, Croesus was informed that the Athenian was held in subjection and torn by faction under Peisistratus, son of Hippocrates, who was at that time tyrant of the Athenians. For to Hippocrates, while he was still a private citizen and had gone to view the Olympic games, a great marvel had occurred. After he had offered the sacrifice, the cauldrons standing upon the hearth, full of pieces of flesh and of water, boiled over without fire beneath them and ran over. Chilon the Lacedaemonian, who happened to be present and to have seen the marvel, advised Hippocrates first not to bring into his house a wife to bear him children, and secondly, if he already had one, to dismiss her, and, if he happened to have a son, to disown him. But Hippocrates, they say, was not willing to be persuaded by Chilon’s advice. And so afterward this Peisistratus was born to him. When the Athenians of the shore were at feud with those of the plain—Megacles, son of Alcmaeon, being leader of the first faction, and Lycurgus, son of Aristolaidas, leader of that of the plain—Peisistratus aimed at the tyranny for himself and gathered a third party. After he had collected supporters and proclaimed himself leader of the men of the mountain-lands, he contrived the following device: he inflicted wounds upon himself and upon his mules, and then drove his chariot into the market-place as if he had just escaped from his enemies, who, he alleged, had tried to kill him as he was driving into the country. He then begged of the commons that he might obtain some protection from them, for before this he had gained a reputation in his command against the Megarians, during which he took Nisaea and performed other signal services. The commons of the Athenians, being deceived, gave him a body of men chosen from among the dwellers in the city—men who became not the spearmen of Peisistratus but his club-men, for they followed behind him bearing wooden clubs. These rose up with Peisistratus and seized the Acropolis. Then Peisistratus became ruler of the Athenians, without disturbing the existing magistrates or changing the ancient laws; but he administered the State under that constitution which was already established, ordering it fairly and well.

The Spartan sage who warned Hippocrates, after the cauldron boiled over without a fire, not to have a son.

## modern-en · cypselus · 14:0

In this way the Mermnadae gained the throne, ousting the Heraclids. When Gyges became ruler, he sent a good many votive offerings to Delphi — among all the silver offerings there, his are more numerous than any other man's, and besides the silver he dedicated a vast quantity of gold, and in particular one offering worth mentioning above the rest: six golden mixing-bowls, dedicated there as his gift. Their weight is thirty talents, and they stand in the treasury of the Corinthians — though in truth this treasury does not belong to the Corinthian state, but to Cypselus, the son of Eëtion. This Gyges was the first of the barbarians known to us to dedicate votive offerings at Delphi, except for Midas, son of Gordias, king of Phrygia, who dedicated the royal throne on which he sat in judgment — a throne well worth seeing, and it stands in the same place as the bowls of Gyges. The gold and silver Gyges dedicated is called Gygian by the Delphians, after the name of the giver.

## modern-en · thales · 73:0

After this, since Alyattes would not give up the Scythians when Cyaxares demanded them, war arose between the Lydians and the Medes that lasted five years. In those years the Medes often defeated the Lydians and the Lydians often defeated the Medes, and they fought, among other engagements, a battle by night. And as they still carried on the war with equally balanced fortune, in the sixth year a battle took place in which, when the fight had begun, it happened that day suddenly turned to night. This change of day Thales of Miletus had foretold to the Ionians, setting as its limit this very year in which the change took place. The Lydians and Medes, when they saw that night had come in place of day, ceased fighting and both became much more eager that peace should be made between them. Those who brought about the peace between them were Syennesis the Cilician and Labynetus the Babylonian. These were the men who urged them to take the oaths, and they also arranged an exchange of marriages: they determined that Alyattes should give his daughter Aryenis to Astyages, son of Cyaxares, since without the compulsion of a strong tie agreements tend not to hold firmly together. Now these nations observe the same ceremonies in the taking of oaths as the Greeks do, and in addition they make an incision in the skin of their arms and then lick up the blood, each from the other.

Thales of Miletus, who foretold the year of the eclipse that stopped the battle between the Medes and the Lydians, and who is said to have diverted the Halys so Croesus could cross.

## modern-en · bias · 27:0

When the Hellenes of Asia had been conquered and forced to pay tribute, he intended next to build ships and attack those who lived on the islands. All was ready for shipbuilding when — so they say — Bias of Priene (or, according to another version, Pittacus of Mytilene) came to Sardis. Asked by Croesus whether there was any news from Hellas, he stopped the shipbuilding with this reply. “O king,” he said, “the men of the islands are hiring a force of ten thousand horsemen, and with them they mean to march on Sardis and fight against you.” Croesus, supposing the report to be true, said, “May the gods put it into the minds of the islanders to come on horseback against the sons of the Lydians!” The other answered, “O king, I see that you greatly desire to catch the men of the islands riding on horses on the mainland, and there is nothing unreasonable in that wish. But what do you think the islanders have been wishing and praying for ever since they heard that you were going to build ships against them, except to catch the Lydians at sea, so that they might take vengeance on you for the Hellenes on the mainland, whom you hold enslaved?” Croesus, they say, was greatly pleased with this reply, and, thinking the man had spoken well, he obeyed his suggestion and put a stop to the shipbuilding; and on that basis he formed a friendship with the Ionians who lived on the islands.

Bias of Priene, who talked Croesus out of building a fleet by telling him the islanders were buying horses — and who advised the Ionians, too late, to sail away and found one city in Sardinia.

When they came to Tartessos, they became friends with the king of the Tartessians, whose name was Arganthonios: he ruled over the Tartessians for eighty years and lived altogether one hundred and twenty. With this man the Phocaians became so exceedingly friendly that at first he bade them leave Ionia and dwell wherever they desired in his own land. When he did not persuade the Phocaians to do this, afterwards, hearing from them of the Mede and how his power was increasing, he gave them money to build a wall around their city. King of Tartessos, who reigned eighty years and lived a hundred and twenty, and offered the Phocaeans his whole country to settle in.

## modern-en · amasis · 30:0

So Solon, having left his native country for that reason and for the sake of seeing the world, came to Amasis in Egypt and also to Croesus at Sardis. There he was entertained as a guest by Croesus in the king's palace; and afterwards, on the third or fourth day, at Croesus's order, the servants led Solon around to see the royal treasuries, and they showed him everything — how great and magnificent it all was. After Solon had looked at it all and examined it at leisure, Croesus asked him, “My Athenian guest, much report of you has come to us, both of your wisdom and of your travels — how, in search of wisdom, you have visited many lands to see them. Now, then, a desire has come upon me to ask whether you have seen any man whom you consider the happiest of all men.” He asked this supposing that he himself was the happiest, but Solon, using no flattery and speaking only the truth, said, “Yes, O king — Tellus the Athenian.” Croesus, astonished at this, asked him earnestly, “In what way do you judge Tellus to be the happiest?” Solon replied, “Tellus, in the first place, lived when his native state was prospering, and he had sons who were fine and good, and saw children born to each of them and all of them growing up. And secondly, he had what with us passes for wealth, and he met with the most glorious end of his life: for when the Athenians fought a battle against their neighbors at Eleusis, he came up in support, routed the enemy, and there died a most noble death. The Athenians buried him publicly on the spot where he fell and honored him greatly.”

King of Egypt, named in Book 1 as one of Croesus’s allies and as the man Solon visited.

## modern-en · minos · 170:0

But Harpagos, after subduing Ionia, proceeded to march against the Carians and Caunians and Lykians, taking Ionians and Aiolians with him to help. Of these, the Carians came to the mainland from the islands; for being in olden times subjects of Minos and called Leleges, they used to dwell in the islands, paying no tribute — so far back as I am able to reach by hearsay — but whenever Minos required it, they used to supply his ships with seamen. And since Minos subdued much land and was fortunate in his fighting, the Carian nation was at that time by much the most famous of all nations, together with him. They produced three inventions of which the Hellenes adopted the use: the Carians were the first to set the fashion of fastening crests on helmets, and of making the devices that are put on shields, and these also were the first to make handles for their shields — whereas up to that time all who were accustomed to use shields carried them without handles and with leathern straps to guide them, having them slung about their necks and left shoulders. Then after a long time, the Dorians and Ionians drove the Carians out of the islands, and so they came to the mainland. With respect to the Carians, the Cretans relate that it happened thus; but the Carians themselves do not agree with this account and suppose that they are dwellers on the mainland from the beginning, and that they always went by the same name they have now. As evidence of this, they point to an ancient temple of Carian Zeus at Mylasa, in which the Mysians and Lydians share as brother races of the Carians, for they say that Lydos and Mysos were brothers of Car. These share in it; but those who, being of another race, have come to speak the same language as the Carians, have no share in it.

Son of Europa, who ruled the sea in the old time and whose subjects the Carians were before they came to the mainland.

## modern-en · sarpedon · 172:0

The Lykians, however, have sprung originally from Crete (for in olden times the whole of Crete was possessed by barbarians). When the sons of Europa, Sarpedon and Minos, came to be at variance in Crete about the kingdom, Minos, having got the better in the strife of parties, drove out both Sarpedon himself and those of his party. They, having been expelled, came to the land of Milyas in Asia, for the land which the Lykians now inhabit was anciently called Milyas, and the Milyans were then called Solymoi. While Sarpedon reigned over them, they were called by the name they had when they came there, by which the Lykians are even now called by the neighboring tribes, namely Termilai. But when Lycos the son of Pandion came from Athens to the land of the Termilai and to Sarpedon — having himself been driven out by his brother Aigeus — then by the name taken from Lycos they were after a time called Lykians. The customs they have are partly Cretan and partly Carian; but one custom they have which is peculiar to them and in which they agree with no other people: they call themselves by their mothers and not by their fathers. If one asks his neighbor who he is, he will state his parentage on the mother's side and enumerate his mother's female ancestors. And if a woman who is a citizen marries a slave, the children are accounted of gentle birth; but if a man who is a citizen, though he were the first among them, has a slave for wife or concubine, the children are without civil rights.

Minos’s brother, driven out of Crete, who led the Termilai into the land that became Lycia.

## modern-en · europa · 2:0

This, the Persians say, is how Io came to Egypt — though the Hellenes tell it differently — and this, according to them, was the first injury. Afterwards, they say, some Hellenes (whose nationality they cannot name) put in at the Phoenician city of Tyre and carried off the king's daughter Europa. These must have been Cretans, and so the two sides were even. But after this, they say, the Hellenes committed the second wrong. They sailed in a warship to Aia in Colchis and up the river Phasis, and after completing the other business that had brought them there, they carried off the king's daughter Medea. The king of Colchis sent a herald to Hellas demanding satisfaction for the abduction and the return of his daughter. The Hellenes replied that since the barbarians had given no satisfaction for the abduction of Io the Argive, they would give none for Medea either.

The king’s daughter carried off from Tyre — the second of the four abductions with which Herodotus opens the quarrel between Europe and Asia.

## modern-en · io · 1:0

The learned among the Persians say the Phoenicians were the ones who started the quarrel. They came, so the story goes, from what is called the Erythraean Sea to the Mediterranean, and once they had settled in the land where they still live today, they immediately began making long sea voyages. Carrying Egyptian and Assyrian merchandise, they traded in many places, and eventually reached Argos — which at that time was the leading state in what is now called Hellas. When the Phoenicians arrived at Argos, they began selling their cargo. On the fifth or sixth day, when nearly all their goods had been sold, a great crowd of women came down to the shore, and among them was the king's daughter, whose name, as the Hellenes also agree, was Io, daughter of Inachus. The women were standing near the ship's stern, buying whatever pleased them most, when the Phoenicians, passing the word among themselves, suddenly rushed at them. Most of the women escaped by running, but Io and a few others were seized, thrown aboard the ship, and carried off at once to Egypt.

Daughter of Inachus, carried off from Argos by Phoenician traders — the first abduction, and the beginning of the whole business.

## modern-en · inachus · 1:0

The learned among the Persians say the Phoenicians were the ones who started the quarrel. They came, so the story goes, from what is called the Erythraean Sea to the Mediterranean, and once they had settled in the land where they still live today, they immediately began making long sea voyages. Carrying Egyptian and Assyrian merchandise, they traded in many places, and eventually reached Argos — which at that time was the leading state in what is now called Hellas. When the Phoenicians arrived at Argos, they began selling their cargo. On the fifth or sixth day, when nearly all their goods had been sold, a great crowd of women came down to the shore, and among them was the king's daughter, whose name, as the Hellenes also agree, was Io, daughter of Inachus. The women were standing near the ship's stern, buying whatever pleased them most, when the Phoenicians, passing the word among themselves, suddenly rushed at them. Most of the women escaped by running, but Io and a few others were seized, thrown aboard the ship, and carried off at once to Egypt.

Io’s father, king of Argos.

## modern-en · medea · 2:0

This, the Persians say, is how Io came to Egypt — though the Hellenes tell it differently — and this, according to them, was the first injury. Afterwards, they say, some Hellenes (whose nationality they cannot name) put in at the Phoenician city of Tyre and carried off the king's daughter Europa. These must have been Cretans, and so the two sides were even. But after this, they say, the Hellenes committed the second wrong. They sailed in a warship to Aia in Colchis and up the river Phasis, and after completing the other business that had brought them there, they carried off the king's daughter Medea. The king of Colchis sent a herald to Hellas demanding satisfaction for the abduction and the return of his daughter. The Hellenes replied that since the barbarians had given no satisfaction for the abduction of Io the Argive, they would give none for Medea either.

The king of Colchis’s daughter, carried off by Greeks in a warship up the Phasis — the third abduction.

## modern-en · helen · 3:0

In the next generation, the Persians say, Alexander, the son of Priam, heard of these events and wanted to get a wife for himself out of Hellas by force, certain that he would never be required to give satisfaction, since the Hellenes had given none themselves. So he carried off Helen. The Hellenes decided first to send messengers demanding her back along with compensation for the abduction. When they pressed this demand, the Trojans threw the abduction of Medea back at them, pointing out that the Hellenes were now demanding from others what they themselves had refused to give, and had not surrendered the woman when she was demanded of them.

Carried off by Alexander, which the Persians say was the fourth abduction and the one the Greeks made a war of, for a single woman.

## modern-en · midas · 14:0

In this way the Mermnadae gained the throne, ousting the Heraclids. When Gyges became ruler, he sent a good many votive offerings to Delphi — among all the silver offerings there, his are more numerous than any other man's, and besides the silver he dedicated a vast quantity of gold, and in particular one offering worth mentioning above the rest: six golden mixing-bowls, dedicated there as his gift. Their weight is thirty talents, and they stand in the treasury of the Corinthians — though in truth this treasury does not belong to the Corinthian state, but to Cypselus, the son of Eëtion. This Gyges was the first of the barbarians known to us to dedicate votive offerings at Delphi, except for Midas, son of Gordias, king of Phrygia, who dedicated the royal throne on which he sat in judgment — a throne well worth seeing, and it stands in the same place as the bowls of Gyges. The gold and silver Gyges dedicated is called Gygian by the Delphians, after the name of the giver.

King of Phrygia, son of Gordias, the only barbarian before Gyges to dedicate offerings at Delphi — his throne.

Grievously troubled by the misfortune, he called upon Zeus the Purifier, protesting to him what he had suffered at his guest’s hands, and he called moreover upon the Protector of Suppliants and the Guardian of Friendship—naming the same god each time—calling upon him as Protector of Suppliants because, when he had received the guest into his house, he had been unknowingly nourishing the slayer of his son, and as Guardian of Friendship because, having sent him as a protector, he had found him to be the worst of foes.”

Invoked in Book 1 as the Purifier, the Protector of Suppliants and the Guardian of Friendship, all three by Adrastus over the body of Croesus’s son.

## modern-en · hera · 31:0

After Solon had moved Croesus to ask further with his story about Tellus—recounting how many marks of happiness the man possessed—the king asked again whom he had judged worthy to be placed next after him, supposing that he himself would certainly win at least the second place. But Solon replied: “Cleobis and Biton. These men, Argives by birth, had wealth enough to live on and, in addition, bodily strength such as I shall describe. Both had equally won prizes in the games, and the following tale is also told of them. There was a festival of Hera among the Argives, and it was absolutely necessary that their mother should be carried in a cart to the temple. But the oxen were not brought up from the field in time. Barred by the shortage of time from any other solution, the young men put themselves under the yoke and drew the wagon, their mother riding upon it. So they pulled it for forty-five stades and arrived at the temple. After they had accomplished this and had been seen by the gathered crowd, a most excellent ending was given to their lives, and in this matter the deity showed that it is better for a man to die than to continue living. For the Argive men stood around praising the strength of the young men, and the Argive women praised the mother for having been lucky enough to bear such sons. The mother, overjoyed both by the deed itself and by the reports of it, took her stand before the image of the goddess and prayed that she would grant to her sons Cleobis and Biton, who had so greatly honored her, the gift that is best for a man to receive. After this prayer, once the young men had sacrificed and feasted, they lay down to sleep inside the temple itself and never rose again, but were held fast in that final ending. The Argives had statues made in their likeness and dedicated them as offerings at Delphi, believing that they had proved themselves most excellent men.”

At whose Argive festival Cleobis and Biton pulled the cart, and whose priestess prayed for the best thing a man can have.

## modern-en · heracles · 7:0

The sovereignty that had belonged to the Heraclids passed to the family of Croesus, called the Mermnadae, in the following way. Candaules — whom the Hellenes call Myrsilus — was the ruler of Sardis and a descendant of Alcaeus, son of Heracles. For Agron, the son of Ninus, son of Belus, son of Alcaeus, was the first of the Heraclids to become king of Sardis, and Candaules, the son of Myrsus, was the last. The kings of this land before Agron were descended from Lydus the son of Atys, from whom the whole people took the name Lydian, having earlier been called Meonian. From these kings the Heraclids — descended from Heracles and the slave-girl of Iardanus — inherited the throne in obedience to an oracle, and they reigned for twenty-two generations, five hundred and five years, passing the power from father to son down to Candaules, the son of Myrsus.

Ancestor, through Alcaeus, of the old kings of Sardis.

## modern-en · lydians · 6:0

Croesus was a Lydian by race, the son of Alyattes, and ruler of the nations that live this side of the river Halys — the river that flows from the south between the Syrians and the Paphlagonians and runs out northward into the sea called the Euxine. This Croesus was the first of the barbarians we know of to subdue certain Hellenes and force them to pay tribute, while others he won over as friends. Those he subdued were the Ionians, Aeolians, and Dorians who live in Asia; those he made his friends were the Lacedaemonians. Before the reign of Croesus, all the Hellenes had been free, for the Cimmerian invasion of Ionia, which came before his time, had not been a conquest of the cities but only a raid for plunder.

Croesus’s people, the first to strike coin and to keep retail shops, and the first barbarians the Greeks paid tribute to.

## modern-en · babylonians · 73:0

After this, since Alyattes would not give up the Scythians when Cyaxares demanded them, war arose between the Lydians and the Medes that lasted five years. In those years the Medes often defeated the Lydians and the Lydians often defeated the Medes, and they fought, among other engagements, a battle by night. And as they still carried on the war with equally balanced fortune, in the sixth year a battle took place in which, when the fight had begun, it happened that day suddenly turned to night. This change of day Thales of Miletus had foretold to the Ionians, setting as its limit this very year in which the change took place. The Lydians and Medes, when they saw that night had come in place of day, ceased fighting and both became much more eager that peace should be made between them. Those who brought about the peace between them were Syennesis the Cilician and Labynetus the Babylonian. These were the men who urged them to take the oaths, and they also arranged an exchange of marriages: they determined that Alyattes should give his daughter Aryenis to Astyages, son of Cyaxares, since without the compulsion of a strong tie agreements tend not to hold firmly together. Now these nations observe the same ceremonies in the taking of oaths as the Greeks do, and in addition they make an incision in the skin of their arms and then lick up the blood, each from the other.

The people of the greatest city Herodotus knows, taken by Cyrus when he drew off the Euphrates and walked in along the riverbed while they were at a festival.

## modern-en · milesians · 17:0

He carried on the war with the Milesians, having inherited it from his father. He would invade their territory and besiege Miletus in this fashion: whenever the crops were ripe in the fields, he marched his army into their country to the sound of pipes, harps, and flutes of both male and female tone. When he reached Milesian land, he did not tear down the farmhouses, burn them, or rip off their doors — he let them stand just as they were. But the trees and the crops on the land he destroyed, and then retreated the way he had come. Since the Milesians controlled the sea, it was useless for his army to lay siege. He spared the houses so that the Milesians would have places to live while they sowed and tilled their fields, and so that through their labor he would have something to destroy the next time he invaded.

## modern-en · caunians · 170:0

But Harpagos, after subduing Ionia, proceeded to march against the Carians and Caunians and Lykians, taking Ionians and Aiolians with him to help. Of these, the Carians came to the mainland from the islands; for being in olden times subjects of Minos and called Leleges, they used to dwell in the islands, paying no tribute — so far back as I am able to reach by hearsay — but whenever Minos required it, they used to supply his ships with seamen. And since Minos subdued much land and was fortunate in his fighting, the Carian nation was at that time by much the most famous of all nations, together with him. They produced three inventions of which the Hellenes adopted the use: the Carians were the first to set the fashion of fastening crests on helmets, and of making the devices that are put on shields, and these also were the first to make handles for their shields — whereas up to that time all who were accustomed to use shields carried them without handles and with leathern straps to guide them, having them slung about their necks and left shoulders. Then after a long time, the Dorians and Ionians drove the Carians out of the islands, and so they came to the mainland. With respect to the Carians, the Cretans relate that it happened thus; but the Carians themselves do not agree with this account and suppose that they are dwellers on the mainland from the beginning, and that they always went by the same name they have now. As evidence of this, they point to an ancient temple of Carian Zeus at Mylasa, in which the Mysians and Lydians share as brother races of the Carians, for they say that Lydos and Mysos were brothers of Car. These share in it; but those who, being of another race, have come to speak the same language as the Carians, have no share in it.

Neighbours of the Carians who say they came from Crete, and who once drove out foreign gods by beating the air with their spears as far as the border.

The people the Greeks were before they were Greeks — whose language Herodotus cannot determine except by what their descendants still speak.

As Cyrus attempted to cross the Gyndes, which was deep enough to be navigable, one of his sacred white horses charged into the river and tried to swim across. The current swept the horse under and carried it away. Cyrus was furious at the river for this outrage. He swore he would make it so weak that women could cross it without getting their knees wet. He suspended his march on Babylon, divided his army in two, and had them dig one hundred and eighty channels on each side of the river—three hundred and sixty in all—fanning out in every direction.
