Then after a long time, the Dorians and Ionians drove the Carians out of the islands, and so they came to the mainland. With respect to the Carians, the Cretans relate that it happened thus; but the Carians themselves do not agree with this account and suppose that they are dwellers on the mainland from the beginning, and that they always went by the same name they have now. As evidence of this, they point to an ancient temple of Carian Zeus at Mylasa, in which the Mysians and Lydians share as brother races of the Carians, for they say that Lydos and Mysos were brothers of Car. These share in it; but those who, being of another race, have come to speak the same language as the Carians, have no share in it.

The name the Carians went by while they were islanders and subjects of Minos.

## modern-en · min · 125:0

Now there was a certain region of the Persian land overgrown with thorns, extending some eighteen or twenty furlongs in each direction. When all had come with what they had been commanded to bring, Cyrus ordered them to clear this region for cultivation within one day. When the Persians had achieved the task set before them, he ordered them to come to him on the next day bathed and clean. Meanwhile Cyrus, having gathered in one place all the flocks of goats and sheep and herds of cattle belonging to his father, slaughtered them and prepared with them to entertain the host of the Persians, and moreover with wine and other provisions of the most agreeable kind. So when the Persians came the next day, he made them recline in a meadow and feasted them. When they had finished dinner, Cyrus asked them whether what they had on the former day or what they had now seemed preferable to them. They said the difference was great, for the former day had for them nothing but evil, and the present day nothing but good. Taking up this saying, Cyrus proceeded to lay bare his whole design, saying: “Men of the Persians, thus it is with you. If you will do as I say, you shall have these and ten thousand other good things, with no servile labor; but if you will not, you shall have labors like that of yesterday, innumerable. Now therefore do as I say and make yourselves free: for I seem to myself to have been born by providential fortune to take these matters in hand, and I think you are not worse men than the Medes, either in other matters or in those which have to do with war. Consider, then, that this is so, and revolt from Astyages at once.”

The first man to be king of Egypt, who dammed the Nile away from Memphis and founded the city on the reclaimed ground. The older translation spells him Men.

## modern-en · nitocris · 184:0

The queen who ruled after her, named Nitocris, was far wiser. She left behind monuments I will describe. She also saw that the Median empire was growing powerful and restless, having already captured Nineveh among other cities. She took every possible precaution against them. First, she diverted the river Euphrates, which had previously run straight through the center of the city. By digging channels upstream, she made it wind so much that it passed one particular Assyrian village three times. (The village is called Ardericca, and anyone sailing down the Euphrates from the Mediterranean still passes this village three times on three separate days.) She also raised high embankments on both sides of the Euphrates. In addition, she dug a vast lake above the city, a short distance from the river, deep enough to reach water and with a perimeter of some fifty-two miles. She used the excavated earth to raise the river embankments even higher. As the lake was dug, she had enormous stones brought to the edge. When all this was finished—the lake dug and the embankments built—she diverted the river into the new lake, filling it. While the old riverbed was empty, she accomplished two things: she lined both banks of the river through the city with baked brick, matching the riverside walls, and she built a bridge over the Euphrates from stones she had quarried for the lake, binding them together with iron and lead. During the day, square wooden platforms were laid across the stones for people to walk on. At night, the platforms were removed to prevent the inhabitants of the two halves of the city from crossing over and robbing each other. When the lake was full and the bridge finished, she turned the river back into its original course from the lake. The lake, now a marsh, served its purpose, and the citizens had a bridge.

The one queen in the priests’ list of three hundred and thirty kings, who avenged her murdered brother by drowning his killers at a feast and then threw herself into a room full of ashes.

## modern-en · moeris-king · 221:0

As further evidence about this land, the priests told me that in the reign of King Moeris, whenever the river rose at least eight cubits, it flooded all of Egypt below Memphis. At the time they told me this, fewer than nine hundred years had passed since Moeris' death. Yet now, unless the river rises at least fifteen or sixteen cubits, it does not overflow. If this land continues to grow in height at the same rate, and to increase in extent in the same proportion, it seems to me that the Egyptians who live below Lake Moeris—especially those in the Delta—will eventually suffer permanent drought as the Nile can no longer reach their fields.

The king before whose reign the Nile needed only eight cubits to flood Egypt, and whose name the great lake carries. The two editions spell the lake Moeris and Moiris.

## modern-en · sesostris · 317:0

Therefore, passing these by, I shall make mention of the king who came after these, whose name was Sesostris. He (the priests said) first of all set out with ships of war from the Arabian gulf and subdued those who dwelt along the shores of the Erythraean Sea, until as he sailed he came to a sea that could no longer be navigated by reason of shoals. Then, after he had returned to Egypt, according to the priests' report, he took a great army and marched over the continent, subduing every nation that stood in his way. Those whom he found valiant and fighting desperately for their freedom — in their lands he set up pillars that told by inscriptions his own name and the name of his country, and how he had subdued them by his power. But as to those whose cities he obtained possession of without fighting or with ease, on their pillars he inscribed words of the same tenor as those for the nations that had shown themselves courageous, and in addition he carved on them the hidden parts of a woman, wishing to signify by this that the people were cowards and effeminate.

The conqueror who set out with a fleet from the Arabian gulf, marched through Asia into Europe, and left carved pillars behind him — with a woman’s parts cut on them where the people gave in without a fight.

## modern-en · pheros · 326:0

Now after Sesostris had ended his life, his son Pheros, they told me, received the kingdom in succession; and he made no warlike expedition, but it chanced to him to become blind by reason of the following accident. When the river had come down in flood, rising to a height of eighteen cubits (higher than ever before), and had gone over the fields, a wind fell upon it and the river became agitated by waves; and this king (they say), moved by presumptuous folly, took a spear and cast it into the midst of the eddies of the stream; and immediately upon this he had a disease of the eyes and was by it made blind. For ten years, then, he was blind, and in the eleventh year there came to him an oracle from the city of Buto, saying that the time of his punishment had expired, and that he should see again if he washed his eyes with the water of a woman who had lain only with her own husband and had not known other men. First he made trial of his own wife, and then, as he continued blind, he went on to try all the women in turn; and when he had at last regained his sight, he gathered together all the women with whom he had made the trial (except her by whose means he had regained his sight) into one city, which is now named Erythrabolus, and having gathered them there he consumed them all by fire, as well as the city itself. As for her by whose means he had regained his sight, he had her himself to wife. Then, after he had escaped the malady of his eyes, he dedicated offerings at each of the temples of renown, and especially (to mention only the most worthy of mention) he dedicated at the temple of the Sun works that are worth seeing — namely two obelisks of stone, each of a single block, measuring a hundred cubits each in length and eight cubits in breadth.

Sesostris’s son, blinded for throwing a spear into the flooding Nile, and cured after ten years by a remedy that cost the lives of a great many women.

## modern-en · proteus-egypt · 327:0

After him, they said, there succeeded to the throne a man of Memphis, whose name in the tongue of the Hellenes was Proteus; for whom there is now a sacred enclosure at Memphis, very fair and well ordered, lying on that side of the temple of Hephaestus which faces the North Wind. Round about this enclosure dwell Phoenicians of Tyre, and this whole region is called the Camp of the Tyrians. Within the enclosure of Proteus there is a temple called the temple of the “foreign Aphrodite,” which I conjecture to be one of Helen the daughter of Tyndareus — not only because I have heard the tale how Helen dwelt with Proteus, but also especially because it is called by the name of the “foreign Aphrodite,” for no other temples of Aphrodite elsewhere have the addition of the word “foreign” to the name.

The king of Memphis who held Helen and the stolen treasure in Egypt for the whole length of the Trojan war, and sent Alexander away — the story Herodotus believes over Homer’s.

## modern-en · thonis · 328:0

And the priests told me, when I inquired, that the events concerning Helen happened thus. Alexander, having carried off Helen, was sailing away from Sparta to his own land, and when he had come to the Aegean Sea contrary winds drove him from his course into the Sea of Egypt; and after that, since the blasts did not cease to blow, he came to Egypt itself — and in Egypt to what is now named the Canobic mouth of the Nile and to Taricheiai. Now there was upon the shore, as still there is now, a temple of Heracles, in which if any man's slave takes refuge and has the sacred marks set upon him, giving himself over to the god, it is not lawful to lay hands upon him; and this custom has continued unchanged from the beginning down to my own time. Accordingly, the attendants of Alexander, having heard of the custom that existed about the temple, ran away from him, and sitting down as suppliants of the god, accused Alexander — because they wished to do him harm — telling the whole tale of how things were about Helen and about the wrong done to Menelaus; and they made this accusation not only to the priests but also to the warden of this river-mouth, whose name was Thonis.

The warden of the Canobic mouth who arrested Alexander’s servants and reported him to Proteus.

## modern-en · rhampsinitos · 336:0

After Proteus, they told me, Rhampsinitos succeeded to the kingdom, and he left as his memorial the gateway to the temple of Hephaistos that faces westward. In front of the gateway he set up two statues, each twenty-five cubits tall. The Egyptians call the one on the north side Summer, and the one on the south side Winter; to Summer they pay reverence and make offerings, while to Winter they do the opposite. (a) This king, they said, gained great wealth in silver, more than any king born after him could match or even approach. Wishing to keep his treasure safe, he had a stone chamber built, with one of its walls forming part of the outside of his palace. The builder, however, had a design upon it, and he contrived as follows: he set one of the stones in such a way that it could easily be removed from the wall by two men, or even by a single man. So when the chamber was finished and the king stored his money inside it, the builder, after some time, drawing near the end of his life, called his two sons to him. He told them how he had arranged the matter when building the king's treasury, all in forethought for them, so that they might have ample means of living. He gave them the precise measurements for removing the stone, telling them that if they paid heed to this, they would be the stewards of the king's treasury. Then he died, and his sons made no long delay in setting to work. They went to the palace by night, found the stone in the wall of the chamber, dealt with it easily, and carried away a great quantity of the wealth within. (b) When the king happened to open the chamber, he was astonished to see the vessels falling short of their full amount, and he did not know whom to blame, since the seals were unbroken and the chamber had been shut close. But when, on opening the chamber a second time and a third, the money was each time seen to be diminished — for the thieves did not slacken in their assaults — he did as follows. He had traps made and set them around the vessels in which the money was kept; and when the thieves came as before and one of them entered, as soon as he came near a vessel he was caught fast in the trap. Realizing how desperate his case was, he immediately called his brother, showed him what had happened, and bade him climb in as quickly as possible and cut off his head, lest he be seen and recognized, bringing destruction upon his brother as well. To the other this seemed sound advice, and he was persuaded; he did so, and fitting the stone back into its place, he departed home carrying his brother's head with him. (c) When day came, the king entered the chamber and was utterly amazed to see the body of the thief held in the trap without its head, the chamber unbroken, and no way to come in or go out. Being at a loss, he hung the dead body up on the wall and set guards there, with orders to seize anyone seen weeping or lamenting and bring him before the king. When the body had been hung up, the mother was deeply grieved. Speaking with her surviving son, she ordered him to contrive, by whatever means he could, to take down and bring home the body of his dead brother; and if he failed to do this, she earnestly threatened that she would go and inform the king that he had the money. (d) Since the mother dealt harshly with the surviving son, and he could not persuade her with his many arguments, he contrived a device for his purpose. He provided himself with asses, filled some skins with wine, loaded them upon the asses, and drove them along. When he came opposite to those guarding the corpse, he pulled out two or three of the necks of the skins and loosened the cords with which they were tied. As the wine ran out, he began to beat his head and cry out loudly, as if he did not know which of the asses to turn to first. When the guards saw the wine flowing out in streams, they ran together onto the road with drinking vessels in their hands and collected the wine that was spilling, counting it pure gain. He railed at them all violently, pretending to be angry, and when the guards tried to appease him, after a while he feigned to be pacified and to abate his anger. At last he drove his asses out of the road and began to set their loads right. Then more talk arose among them, one or two of them made jokes at him and brought him to laugh with them, and in the end he made them a present of one of the skins as well. At that they lay down then and there without further ado, intending to drink, and they took him into their company and invited him to stay with them and join them in their drinking. So he, as one might expect, was persuaded and stayed. As they welcomed him in friendly fashion as they drank, he made them a present of another skin. So at length, having drunk liberally, the guards became completely drunk, and being overcome by sleep they went to bed on the very spot where they had been drinking. He then, since it was now far on in the night, first took down the body of his brother, and then in mockery shaved the right cheeks of all the guards. After that he placed the dead body upon the asses and drove them home, having accomplished what his mother had enjoined upon him. (e) When it was reported to the king that the dead body of the thief had been stolen away, he displayed great anger; and desiring above all to find out who it could be that devised these things, he did this — so at least they said, though I do not believe the account. He caused his own daughter to sit in a brothel and ordered her to receive all comers equally, and before lying with any of them she was to compel each one to tell her what was the most cunning and the most unholy deed he had done in all his life; and whoever should relate what had happened concerning the thief, she must seize him and not let him go. So she did as her father had enjoined. The thief, hearing the purpose for which this was done and wishing to outdo the king in cleverness, did this. From the body of one lately dead he cut off the arm at the shoulder and went with it under his cloak. Having gone in to the king's daughter and being asked the same question as the others, he related that he had done the most unholy deed when he cut off the head of his brother caught in a trap in the king's treasure-chamber, and the most cunning deed when he made the guards drunk and took down the dead body of his brother that had been hung up. When she heard this and tried to lay hold of him, the thief held out to her in the darkness the arm of the corpse, which she grasped and held, thinking she was holding the man's own arm. But the thief left it in her hands and departed, escaping through the door. (f) When this also was reported to the king, he was at first amazed at the ready invention and daring of the fellow. Then afterwards he sent round to all the cities and made proclamation, granting a free pardon to the thief and promising a great reward as well if he would come into his presence. The thief, trusting to the proclamation, came to the king, and Rhampsinitos marvelled greatly at him and gave him this daughter of his to wife, considering him to be the most knowing of all men. For just as the Egyptians were distinguished from all other men, so was he distinguished from the other Egyptians.

The rich king whose treasury a mason’s two sons robbed through a movable stone — the best story in Book 2 — and who ended by marrying his daughter to the thief.

## modern-en · cheops · 339:0

Down to the time when Rhampsinitos was king, they told me, there was nothing in Egypt but orderly rule, and Egypt prospered greatly. But after him Cheops became king and brought them to every kind of misery. He shut up all the temples, and having first stopped them from sacrificing there, he then ordered all the Egyptians to work for him. Some were assigned to drag stones from the quarries in the Arabian mountains to the Nile; others he ordered to receive the stones after they had been ferried across the river in boats, and to haul them to the so-called Libyan mountains. They worked in shifts of a hundred thousand men at a time, for three months straight. Ten years of this oppression passed while the causeway was built along which they hauled the stones — a causeway that is, in my view, a work not much less than the pyramid itself. Its length is five furlongs, its breadth ten fathoms, and its height, where it is highest, eight fathoms, and it is made of smoothed stone with figures carved upon it. For this, they said, the ten years were spent, as well as for the underground chambers on the hill on which the pyramids stand, which he had made as burial chambers for himself on an island, having led a channel from the Nile to the place. For the construction of the pyramid itself a period of twenty years passed. The pyramid is square, each side measuring eight hundred feet, and its height is the same. It is built of smoothed stones fitted together in the most perfect manner, not one of the stones being less than thirty feet in length.

The king who shut the temples, set the whole people to work on his pyramid for twenty years, and sold his own daughter’s favours to pay for it.

## modern-en · chephren · 342:0

This Cheops, the Egyptians said, reigned for fifty years; and after he died, his brother Chephren succeeded to the kingdom. This king followed the same pattern as the other in every respect, including the fact that he built a pyramid. His pyramid did not reach the dimensions of the one built by his predecessor — I know this, having measured it myself — and moreover it has no underground chambers beneath it, nor does a channel from the Nile flow to it as to the other, where the water coming through a built conduit flows around an island within, and where they say Cheops himself is buried. But for a foundation he laid the first course in Ethiopian stone of varied colors. He built this pyramid forty feet lower than the other in size, placing it next to the great pyramid. The two stand on the same hill, which is about a hundred feet high. And Chephren, they said, reigned fifty-six years.

Cheops’s brother and successor, who ruled in the same manner for fifty-six years; the Egyptians will not willingly say either brother’s name.

## modern-en · mykerinos · 344:0

After him, they said, Mykerinos became king over Egypt — the son of Cheops. His father's deeds displeased him, and he both opened the temples and gave the people, who had been ground down to the last extremity of misery, their liberty to return to their own affairs and to their sacrifices. He also handed down judgments in their lawsuits that were more just than those of all the other kings. For this reason the Egyptians praise this king above all the kings who had arisen in Egypt before him; for he not only gave good decisions, but also, when a man complained of a decision, he paid him compensation out of his own property and so satisfied his desire. But while Mykerinos was acting mercifully to his subjects and conducting himself in the way I have described, calamities befell him. The first was this: his daughter died, the only child he had in his house. Grieved beyond measure by what had happened, and wishing to bury his daughter in a more remarkable manner than others, he made a cow of wood, covered it over with gold, and then buried within it this daughter who, as I said, had died.

Cheops’s son, who opened the temples again and gave the people back their lives — and was told by the oracle that he had six years to live because Egypt was meant to suffer a hundred and fifty. He answered by lighting lamps and living the nights as well.

## modern-en · asychis · 351:0

After Mykerinos, the priests said, Asychis became king of Egypt, and he built for Hephaistos the temple gateway that faces the sunrise — by far the most beautiful and largest of the gateways. While all of them have figures carved upon them and countless other architectural ornaments, this one has them far more than the rest. In this king's reign, they told me, as the circulation of money was very slow, a law was passed for the Egyptians allowing a man to borrow the money he needed by offering as security the dead body of his father. An additional clause was added: the lender was to have a claim on the whole burial chamber belonging to the borrower, and the borrower who offered this security, if he refused to repay the debt, would be subject to this penalty — that neither he himself would be allowed burial when he died, either in the family tomb or any other, nor would he be allowed to bury any kinsman who died. Wishing to surpass the kings of Egypt before him, this king left as his memorial a pyramid that he made of bricks, on which there is an inscription carved in stone that says: “Do not despise me in comparison with the pyramids of stone, for I surpass them as much as Zeus surpasses the other gods. For with a pole they struck into the lake, and whatever mud stuck to the pole, they gathered up and made into bricks, and in this way they finished me.”

The king who made a law that a man might borrow on his father’s corpse, and built a pyramid of brick with an inscription telling it not to compare itself with the stone ones.

## modern-en · anysis · 352:0

and after him reigned a blind man from the city of Anysis, whose name was Anysis. In his reign the Ethiopians, led by their king Sabacos, marched against Egypt with a great host. So this blind man fled and took refuge in the marshlands, and the Ethiopian ruled over Egypt for fifty years, during which he did the following: whenever an Egyptian committed a transgression, he would never put him to death, but he sentenced each man according to the gravity of the offense, ordering them to throw up an embankment before the city each offender came from. In this way the cities were raised still higher than before; for they had first been embanked by those who dug the channels in the reign of Sesostris, and then again in the reign of the Ethiopian, and so they were raised very high. While other cities in Egypt also stood high, I think it was at Bubastis in particular that the earth was piled up most. In this city there is a temple very much worth mentioning, for though there are other larger and more costly temples, none is more pleasing to the eye than this one. Now Bubastis in the Greek tongue is Artemis,

The blind king of the city of the same name, who fled into the marshes when the Ethiopians came and hid there fifty years on a floating island of ash and earth.

and after him reigned a blind man from the city of Anysis, whose name was Anysis. In his reign the Ethiopians, led by their king Sabacos, marched against Egypt with a great host. In this way the cities were raised still higher than before; for they had first been embanked by those who dug the channels in the reign of Sesostris, and then again in the reign of the Ethiopian, and so they were raised very high. In this city there is a temple very much worth mentioning, for though there are other larger and more costly temples, none is more pleasing to the eye than this one. Now Bubastis in the Greek tongue is Artemis,

The Ethiopian who ruled Egypt for fifty years without putting anyone to death, and left because of a dream rather than wait for it to come true.

## modern-en · sethos · 356:0

After him the priest of Hephaistos, whose name was Sethos, came to the throne. This man, they said, neglected and held in no regard the warrior class of the Egyptians, thinking he would have no need of them. Besides other slights he put upon them, he also took from them the plots of grain-land which had been granted to them as a special gift in the reigns of previous kings — twelve plots to each man. After this, Sanacharib, king of the Arabians and Assyrians, marched a great army against Egypt. Then the warriors of the Egyptians refused to come to the rescue, and the priest, being driven into a tight corner, entered the sanctuary of the temple and lamented before the image of the god the danger hanging over him. As he was lamenting, sleep came over him, and in his vision it seemed that the god came and stood beside him and encouraged him, saying he would suffer no harm if he went out to meet the army of the Arabians, for he himself would send him helpers. Trusting in what he had seen in his sleep, he took with him, they said, those Egyptians who were willing to follow, and encamped at Pelusion, for that was the route of the invasion. Not one of the warrior class followed him, but shopkeepers, artisans, and men of the marketplace. Then, after they arrived, field mice swarmed by night upon their enemies and ate through their quivers and their bows, and moreover the handgrips of their shields, so that the next day they fled, and being without weapons to defend themselves, great numbers of them fell. To this day this king stands in the temple of Hephaistos in stone, holding a mouse on his hand, and by letters inscribed he says these words: “Let whoever looks upon me learn to fear the gods.”

The priest of Hephaistos who despised the warrior class and was saved from Sennacherib by field-mice that ate the Assyrians’ bowstrings overnight.

## modern-en · psammetichos · 104:0

From there they went on to invade Egypt; and when they were in Syria, which is called Palestine, Psammetichus, king of Egypt, met them, and by gifts and entreaties he turned them from their purpose so that they should advance no further. As they retreated, when they came to the city of Ascalon in Syria, most of the Scythians passed through without doing any damage, but a few of them who had stayed behind plundered the temple of Aphrodite Urania. This temple, as I find by inquiry, is the most ancient of all the temples that belong to this goddess; for the temple in Cyprus was founded from this one, as the people of Cyprus themselves report, and it was the Phoenicians who founded the temple in Cythera, coming from this land of Syria. So these Scythians who had plundered the temple at Ascalon, and their descendants forever after, were smitten by the divinity with a disease that made them women instead of men. The Scythians say that for this reason they were diseased, and that for this reason travelers who visit Scythia today see among them the affliction of those whom the Scythians call Enareis.

One of the twelve kings, driven out and returned with the bronze men the oracle promised; he reunited Egypt, opened it to Greek traders, and shut two children away with a mute shepherd to find out which language came first. The two editions spell him Psammetichos and Psammetichus.

## modern-en · necos · 367:0

This Psammetichos had previously been a fugitive from the Ethiopian Sabacos, who had killed his father Necos; from him, I say, he had been a fugitive in Syria. When the Ethiopian departed as a result of the vision in the dream, the Egyptians of the district of Saïs brought him back to his own country. Afterwards, when he was king, it was his fate to be a fugitive a second time on account of the helmet, being driven by the eleven kings into the marshland. Holding that he had been gravely wronged by them, he considered how he might take vengeance on those who had driven him out. When he sent to the oracle of Leto in the city of Buto, where the Egyptians have their most truthful oracle, he was given the reply that vengeance would come when men of bronze appeared from the sea. He was strongly inclined not to believe that bronze men would come to help him; but not long afterwards, certain Ionians and Carians who had sailed out for plunder were forced to come ashore in Egypt, and having landed and being clad in bronze armor, one of the Egyptians, never having seen men in bronze armor before, came to the marshland and brought word to Psammetichos that bronze men had come from the sea and were plundering the plain. He, perceiving that the saying of the oracle was coming to pass, dealt in a friendly manner with the Ionians and Carians, and with large promises persuaded them to take his side. Then, having persuaded them, with the help of the Egyptians who favored his cause and of these foreign mercenaries, he overthrew the kings.

Psammetichos’s son, who began the canal to the Red Sea, gave it up when the oracle warned him, and sent Phoenicians to sail round Libya. The older translation spells him Necos; he is also the father Sabacos killed.

## modern-en · psammis · 374:0

After abandoning the canal, Necos turned to warfare. He had triremes built, some for the Mediterranean and some for the Arabian Gulf on the Red Sea. Their ship sheds can still be seen. He used these ships when he needed them. On land, Necos fought and defeated the Syrians at Magdolos. After the battle, he captured the great Syrian city of Cadytis. He dedicated the clothes he wore during these victories to Apollo, sending them to the temple of the Branchidai near Miletos. After a total reign of sixteen years, Necos died and passed the throne to his son Psammis.

Necos’s son, who reigned six years, invaded Ethiopia, and gave the Eleians the obvious answer about their own games.

## modern-en · apries · 376:0

When Psammis had been king of Egypt for only six years and had made an expedition to Ethiopia, and immediately afterwards ended his life, Apries son of Psammis inherited the kingdom in succession. This man came to be the most prosperous of all the kings up to that time, except only his forefather Psammetichos, and he reigned twenty-five years, during which he led an army against Sidon and fought a sea-battle with the king of Tyre. Since, however, it was fated that evil should come upon him, it came through a matter which I shall relate at greater length in the Libyan history, and only briefly here. Apries sent a great expedition against the Kyrenians and met with a correspondingly great disaster; and the Egyptians, considering him to blame for this, revolted against him, supposing that Apries had deliberately sent them out to obvious ruin so that — as they said — there might be a slaughter of them and he might the more securely rule over the rest of the Egyptians. Indignant at this, both the men who had returned from the expedition and the friends of those who had perished openly revolted.

Psammis’s son, the most prosperous king but one until his army went over to Amasis; he was kept alive in his own palace and then handed to the Egyptians, who strangled him.

## modern-en · patarbemis · 377:0

Hearing of this, Apries sent Amasis to them to put a stop to it by persuasion; and when he had come and was trying to restrain the Egyptians, as he was speaking and telling them not to revolt, one of the Egyptians stood up behind him and placed a helmet on his head, saying as he did so that he was putting it on to crown him king. To Amasis what was done was not entirely unwelcome, as he showed by his behavior; for as soon as the rebellious Egyptians had set him up as king, he prepared to march against Apries. Apries, hearing this, sent one of his own attendants to Amasis, a man of reputation whose name was Patarbemis, ordering him to bring Amasis alive into his presence. When Patarbemis came and summoned Amasis, the latter, who happened to be sitting on horseback, lifted up his leg and behaved in an unseemly manner, telling him to take that back to Apries. Nevertheless, they say, Patarbemis pressed him to go to the king, since the king had sent to summon him, and he replied that for some time past he had been preparing to do so, and that Apries would have no cause to fault him. Then Patarbemis, perceiving his intent both from what he said and from seeing his preparations, departed in haste, wishing to inform the king as quickly as possible of what was going on. When he returned to Apries without bringing Amasis, the king, paying no attention to what he said, but moved by violent anger, ordered his ears and nose to be cut off. And the rest of the Egyptians who still remained on his side, when they saw the man of greatest reputation among them suffering such shameful outrage, waited no longer but joined the others in revolt and delivered themselves over to Amasis.

The man of reputation Apries sent to fetch Amasis back, and whose ears and nose Apries cut off when he came back without him — which lost Apries the rest of his subjects.

## modern-en · ladice · 396:0

Amasis also made a pact of friendship and alliance with the people of Cyrene. He decided to take a wife from there—either because he wanted a Greek wife, or simply out of friendship for Cyrene. He married a woman named Ladice, said by some to be the daughter of Battus, by others of Arcesilaus, and by still others of Critobulos, a prominent citizen. When Amasis lay with her, he found himself unable to consummate the marriage, though he had no trouble with his other wives. After this continued for some time, Amasis told Ladice he believed she had used witchcraft on him, and that nothing could save her from the worst death of any woman. Ladice denied everything, but Amasis was unmoved. So she silently vowed to Aphrodite that if Amasis lay with her successfully that night, she would send a statue to the goddess at Cyrene. Immediately after the vow, Amasis was able to perform. From then on, every time he came to her, he succeeded. He loved her very much after that. Ladice fulfilled her vow and sent the statue, which was still in good condition in my time, set up facing outward from the city of Cyrene. When Cambyses conquered Egypt and learned who Ladice was, he sent her home to Cyrene unharmed.

The Cyrenaean woman Amasis married, who prayed to Aphrodite when he could not come to her and afterwards sent the goddess a statue.

## modern-en · amyrtaios · 355:0

Then when the Ethiopian had gone away out of Egypt, the blind man returned from the marshland and began to rule again, having lived for those fifty years on an island he had made by piling up ashes and earth. Whenever any Egyptian visited him bringing food — as each had been appointed to do without the Ethiopian's knowledge — he also bade them bring some ashes as their gift. No one was able to find this island before Amyrtaios; that is, for more than seven hundred years the kings who rose before Amyrtaios were not able to find it. The name of this island is Elbo, and its size is ten furlongs on each side.

The only man in seven hundred years to find the floating island in the marshes where Anysis had hidden.

## modern-en · rhodopis · 349:0

This king also left behind him a pyramid, much smaller than his father's, square in shape and measuring three hundred feet lacking twenty on each side, built moreover of Ethiopian stone up to half its height. This pyramid some of the Greeks claim was built by the courtesan Rhodopis, but in saying this they are not speaking correctly. Besides, it is obvious to me that those who say this do not even know who Rhodopis was — otherwise they would not have attributed to her the building of a pyramid like this, on which, so to speak, countless thousands of talents were spent. Moreover, they do not know that Rhodopis flourished in the reign of Amasis, not in this king's reign, for Rhodopis lived many years later than the kings who left behind the pyramids. By descent she was Thracian, and she was a slave of Iadmon son of Hephaistopolis, a Samian, and a fellow-slave of Aesop the fable-maker — for he too was once the slave of Iadmon, as was proved especially by this fact: when the people of Delphi repeatedly proclaimed, in accordance with an oracle, that someone should claim the blood-money for Aesop's death, no one else came forward, but at length the grandson of Iadmon, also called Iadmon, claimed it; and so it is shown that Aesop too was Iadmon's slave.

The Thracian courtesan the Greeks credit with the third pyramid — wrongly, Herodotus says, since she lived generations later. She spent a tenth of her fortune on iron roasting-spits at Delphi.

## modern-en · charaxos · 350:0

As for Rhodopis, she came to Egypt brought by Xanthes the Samian, and having arrived there to practice her trade, she was ransomed from slavery for a large sum by a man of Mytilene, Charaxos son of Scamandronymos, the brother of Sappho the lyric poet. Thus Rhodopis was set free, and she remained in Egypt, and by her beauty she won so much favor that she made a great deal of money — great for someone like Rhodopis — though not enough to cover the cost of such a pyramid as this. In fact, there is no need to credit her with very great wealth, since the tithe of her wealth can still be seen even now by anyone who wants to look. For Rhodopis wished to leave behind in Hellas a memorial of herself — something that no one else had thought of or dedicated in a temple — and to offer it at Delphi as her memorial. Accordingly, with the tithe of her wealth she had iron spits made, large enough to pierce a whole ox, and many in number, going as far as her tithe allowed. She sent them to Delphi, and they are still lying there today, heaped together behind the altar the Chians dedicated, directly opposite the inner sanctum of the temple. As it happens, the courtesans at Naucratis tend to win renown, for this woman first, about whom this story is told, became so famous that every Greek without exception came to know the name of Rhodopis; and after her, one named Archidiche was sung about throughout Hellas, though she was less talked about than the former. As for Charaxos, after ransoming Rhodopis and returning to Mytilene, he was savagely abused by Sappho in an ode. About Rhodopis, then, I will say no more.

The man of Mytilene who bought Rhodopis’s freedom at great expense, and was mocked for it in his sister’s poetry.

## modern-en · etearchus · 247:0

This much, however, I heard from men of Cyrene, who told me they had gone to the Oracle of Ammon and had come into conversation with Etearchus king of the Ammonians; and it happened that after speaking of other matters they fell to discussing the Nile and how no one knew its sources. Etearchus said that once there had come to him men of the Nasamonians (this is a Libyan people who dwell in the Syrtis, and also in the land east of the Syrtis for no great distance), and when these Nasamonians came, and were asked by him whether they could tell him anything more than he knew about the desert parts of Libya, they said there had been among them certain sons of chief men, who were of unruly disposition; and these, when they grew up to be men, had devised various extravagant undertakings, and among them they drew lots for five of themselves to go and see the desert parts of Libya and try whether they could discover more than those who had previously explored furthest. For in the parts of Libya along the Northern Sea, beginning from Egypt and going as far as the headland of Soloeis (which is the extreme point of Libya), Libyans — and many races of them — extend along the whole coast, except such portions as the Hellenes and Phoenicians hold. But in the upper parts, which lie above the seacoast and above the peoples whose land comes down to the sea, Libya is full of wild beasts; and in the parts beyond the land of wild beasts it is full of sand, terribly waterless, and utterly desert. These young men then (they said), being sent out by their companions well supplied with water and provisions, went first through the inhabited country; after they had passed through this, they came to the country of wild beasts; and after this they passed through the desert, making their way towards the West Wind. Having crossed a great tract of sand in many days, they saw at last trees growing on a level place; and when they came up to them, they began to pluck the fruit that was on the trees. But as they began to pluck it, small men came upon them — of less stature than men of the common size — and these seized them and carried them off. Neither could the Nasamonians understand anything of their speech nor could those who were carrying them off understand the speech of the Nasamonians; and they led them (so it is said) through very great swamps, and after passing through these they came to a city in which all the men were of the same short stature as those who had carried them off, and black of skin. By the city ran a great river, which flowed from the west toward the sunrise, and in it were seen crocodiles.

King of the Ammonians, who told the Nasamonians’ story of the five young men who crossed the desert and came to a city of small black men beside a river full of crocodiles.

## modern-en · archander · 312:0

When the Nile comes over the land, the cities alone are seen rising above the water, resembling more nearly than anything else the islands in the Aegean Sea; for the rest of Egypt becomes a sea, and only the cities rise above the water. Accordingly, whenever this happens, they travel by water — not now by the channels of the river, but over the middle of the plain. For example, as one sails up from Naucratis to Memphis the passage is then close by the pyramids, whereas the usual passage is not the same even here, but goes by the point of the Delta and the city of Cercasorus; while if you sail over the plain to Naucratis from the sea and from Canobus, you will go by Anthylla and the city called after Archander.

Son-in-law of Danaus, after whom a city in the Delta is named; Herodotus doubts the name is Egyptian at all.

## modern-en · hecataeus · 358:0

When the historian Hecataeus visited Thebes and traced his ancestry back to a god in the sixteenth generation, the priests of Zeus did much the same for him as they did for me (though I did not trace my own genealogy). They led him into the great inner hall of the temple and showed him wooden statues—as many as I described. Each chief priest sets up a statue of himself during his lifetime. The priests counted and showed the statues, demonstrating that each was the son of the one before, going through all the statues from the most recent all the way back to the earliest. When Hecataeus traced his descent and connected himself to a god in the sixteenth generation, they challenged him with their own genealogy, refusing to accept that any man was descended from a god. They traced their counter-genealogy like this: each statue, they said, was a piromis born of a piromis (meaning a good and noble man). They showed three hundred and forty-five statues, each piromis descended from piromis, without ever connecting the line to either a god or a hero.

The historian who traced his own descent to a god in the sixteenth generation, and was shown three hundred and forty-five wooden statues at Thebes to correct him.

## modern-en · menelaus · 328:0

And the priests told me, when I inquired, that the events concerning Helen happened thus. Alexander, having carried off Helen, was sailing away from Sparta to his own land, and when he had come to the Aegean Sea contrary winds drove him from his course into the Sea of Egypt; and after that, since the blasts did not cease to blow, he came to Egypt itself — and in Egypt to what is now named the Canobic mouth of the Nile and to Taricheiai. Now there was upon the shore, as still there is now, a temple of Heracles, in which if any man's slave takes refuge and has the sacred marks set upon him, giving himself over to the god, it is not lawful to lay hands upon him; and this custom has continued unchanged from the beginning down to my own time. Accordingly, the attendants of Alexander, having heard of the custom that existed about the temple, ran away from him, and sitting down as suppliants of the god, accused Alexander — because they wished to do him harm — telling the whole tale of how things were about Helen and about the wrong done to Menelaus; and they made this accusation not only to the priests but also to the warden of this river-mouth, whose name was Thonis.

Helen’s husband, who came to Egypt for her after Troy fell, got her back from Proteus, and repaid the Egyptians by sacrificing two of their children for a wind.

## modern-en · hector · 335:0

Thus the priests of the Egyptians told me; and I myself agree with the story told about Helen, adding this consideration: if Helen had been in Ilion, she would have been given up to the Hellenes, whether Alexander consented or no. For assuredly Priam was not so mad, nor the others of his house, as to be willing to run the risk of ruin for themselves, their children, and their city, in order that Alexander might have Helen as his wife. And even supposing that for the first part of the time they had been so inclined, yet when many other Trojans besides were losing their lives as often as they fought with the Hellenes, and when of Priam's own sons two or three or even more were slain in every battle (if one may trust at all the Epic poets) — when, I say, things were coming to such a pass, I believe that even if Priam himself had had Helen as wife, he would have given her back to the Achaeans, if by so doing he might be freed from the evils that oppressed him. Nor was the kingdom coming to Alexander next, so that with Priam old the government was in his hands; but Hector, who was both older and more of a man than he, would have received it after Priam's death, and it would not have befitted Hector to allow his brother to continue his wrongdoing, seeing that great evils were coming to pass on his account both to himself privately and in general to the other Trojans. In truth, however, they lacked the power to give Helen back; and the Hellenes did not believe them, though they spoke the truth — because, as I declare my opinion, the divine power was purposing to cause them utterly to perish, and so make it evident to men that for great wrongs great also are the chastisements that come from the gods. And thus have I delivered my opinion concerning these matters.

Priam’s elder and abler son, whose existence Herodotus uses as the proof that Troy would have given Helen up if it had had her.

## modern-en · homer · 238:0

As for the man who talked about the Ocean, he carried his tale into the region of the unknown, and so he need not be refuted; for I for my part know of no river Ocean existing, but I think that Homer or one of the poets before him invented the name and introduced it into his verse.

Whom Herodotus supposes to have invented the river Ocean, to have known the Egyptian version of Helen and set it aside as less suitable for poetry, and to have lived four hundred years before his own day.

## modern-en · hesiod · 268:0

But whence each of the gods had his birth, or whether they all existed from the beginning, and what forms they have — these things they did not learn until yesterday, so to speak, or the day before. For Hesiod and Homer were, I suppose, four hundred years before my time, and no more; and it is these two who made a theogony for the Hellenes, gave the titles to the gods, assigned them their honours and their arts, and set forth their forms. The poets who are said to have lived before these men were, in my opinion, really after them. Of these things the first are said by the priestesses of Dodona, and the latter — those concerning Hesiod and Homer — by myself.

Named with Homer as the pair who made the Greeks their theogony, gave the gods their titles and described their forms.

## modern-en · perseus · 230:0

If we were to follow the view of the Ionians concerning Egypt — who say that the Delta alone is Egypt, reckoning its seacoast to run from the watchtower called Perseus's to the fish-curing houses of Pelusium, a distance of forty schoeni, and extending inland as far as the city of Cercasorus, where the Nile divides and runs to Pelusium and Canobus, while as for the rest of what we call Egypt, they assign it partly to Libya and partly to Arabia — if, I say, we were to follow this account, we should thereby declare that in former times the Egyptians had no land to live in. For, as we have seen, their Delta at any rate is alluvial, and has only lately come into being (so to speak), as the Egyptians themselves say and as I believe. If then at first there was no land for them to live in, why did they waste their labour trying to prove that they had come into being before all other men? They need not have experimented with children to see what language they would first utter. I am not of the opinion that the Egyptians came into being at the same time as that land the Ionians call the Delta, but rather that they have existed always, ever since the human race came into being, and that as their land advanced forwards, many of them were left in their first dwellings while many came down gradually to the lower parts. At any rate it is certain that in old times Thebes bore the name of Egypt, and the circumference of this region measures six thousand one hundred and twenty furlongs.

Whose watchtower stands on the Egyptian coast and whose sandal turns up at Chemmis; the people there say he was theirs, being descended from Danaus.

## modern-en · danaus · 306:0

Hellenic customs they will by no means follow, and generally speaking they follow those of no other men whatever. This rule is observed by most of the Egyptians; but there is a large city named Chemmis in the Theban district near Neapolis, and in this city there is a temple of Perseus son of Danae, which is square-shaped, and around it grow date-palms. The gateway of the temple is built of stone and of very great size, and at the entrance two great stone statues stand. Within this enclosure is a temple-house, and in it stands an image of Perseus. These people of Chemmis say that Perseus often appears in their land and often within the temple, and that a sandal worn by him is sometimes found, measuring two cubits in length; and whenever this appears, all Egypt prospers. So they say, and they honour Perseus in Hellenic fashion thus: they hold an athletic contest, which includes the whole list of games, and they offer as prizes cattle, cloaks, and skins. When I asked why Perseus appeared to them alone, and why they were separated from all the other Egyptians in holding an athletic contest, they said that Perseus had been born of their city; for Danaus and Lynceus, they said, were men of Chemmis and had sailed to Hellas, and from them they traced a descent down to Perseus. They told me he had come to Egypt for the reason the Hellenes also give — namely to bring the Gorgon's head from Libya — and had then visited them too and recognised all his kinsfolk; and they said he had thoroughly learnt the name of Chemmis before coming to Egypt, since he had heard it from his mother, and that they celebrated an athletic contest for him by his own command.

Of Chemmis, from whom the people there trace Perseus; his daughters brought the rite of the Thesmophoria out of Egypt. The two editions spell him Danaus and Danaos.

## modern-en · amphitryon · 258:0

About Heracles I heard the account that he was one of the twelve gods; but of the other Heracles whom the Hellenes know I was not able to hear anything in any part of Egypt. Moreover, to prove that the Egyptians did not take the name of Heracles from the Hellenes, but rather the Hellenes from the Egyptians — I mean those of the Hellenes who gave the name Heracles to the son of Amphitryon — of this, besides many other proofs, there is chiefly this: that the parents of this Heracles, Amphitryon and Alcmene, were both of Egyptian descent; and also that the Egyptians say they know neither the name of Poseidon nor of the Dioscuri, nor have these been accepted by them as gods among their other gods. Yet if they had received the name of any divinity from the Hellenes, they would naturally have preserved the memory of these most of all — assuming, as I suppose and as my judgment compels me to think, that in those times, as now, some of the Hellenes made voyages and were seafaring folk; so that the Egyptians would have learnt the names of these gods even more than that of Heracles. In fact, however, Heracles is a very ancient Egyptian god; and (as the Egyptians themselves say) it is seventeen thousand years to the beginning of the reign of Amasis from the time when the twelve gods — of whom they count Heracles one — were begotten of the eight gods.

Heracles’s mortal father — of Egyptian descent, the Egyptians say, which is why the Greeks are wrong about which Heracles is older.

## modern-en · melampus · 264:0

Now I think that Melampus the son of Amytheon was not without knowledge of these rites of sacrifice, but was acquainted with them. For Melampus is the one who first set forth to the Hellenes the name of Dionysus and the manner of sacrifice and the procession of the phallos. Strictly speaking, when he made it known he did not take in the whole of it; but those wise men who came after him made it known more at large. Melampus, then, is the one who taught about the phallos that is carried in procession for Dionysus; and from him the Hellenes learnt to do what they do. I say, then, that Melampus, being a man of ability, devised for himself an art of divination, and having learnt from Egypt he taught the Hellenes many things, and among them those that concern Dionysus, making changes in some few points of them. For I shall not say that what is done in the worship of the god in Egypt came accidentally to be the same as what is done among the Hellenes, for then these rites would have been in character with the Hellenic worship and not lately brought in; nor indeed shall I say that the Egyptians took either this or any other customary observance from the Hellenes. But I think it most probable that Melampus learnt the matters concerning Dionysus from Cadmus the Tyrian and from those who came with him from Phoenicia to the land we now call Boeotia.

Son of Amytheon, who Herodotus thinks brought the rite of Dionysus into Greece from Egypt, having learned it from Cadmus and the Phoenicians.

I say, then, that Melampus, being a man of ability, devised for himself an art of divination, and having learnt from Egypt he taught the Hellenes many things, and among them those that concern Dionysus, making changes in some few points of them. For I shall not say that what is done in the worship of the god in Egypt came accidentally to be the same as what is done among the Hellenes, for then these rites would have been in character with the Hellenic worship and not lately brought in; nor indeed shall I say that the Egyptians took either this or any other customary observance from the Hellenes. But I think it most probable that Melampus learnt the matters concerning Dionysus from Cadmus the Tyrian and from those who came with him from Phoenicia to the land we now call Boeotia.

Melampus’s father.

## modern-en · cadmus · 55:0

These lines, when they reached him, pleased Croesus more than all the rest, for he supposed that a mule would never be ruler of the Medes in place of a man, and that consequently he himself and his heirs would never cease from their rule. After this he turned his thoughts to inquiring which of the Greek peoples he should esteem the most powerful and win over to himself as friends. On inquiring he found that the Lacedaemonians and the Athenians had the pre-eminence, the first among the Dorian and the others among the Ionian race. For these were the most eminent peoples in ancient times, the second being a Pelasgian and the first a Hellenic race. The one never migrated from its original seat in any direction, while the other was exceedingly given to wanderings. For in the reign of Deucalion this race dwelt in Phthiotis, and in the time of Dorus, son of Hellen, in the land lying below Ossa and Olympus, which is called Histiaeotis. When it was driven out of Histiaeotis by the sons of Cadmus, it dwelt in Pindus and was called Macednian; from there it moved afterward to Dryopis, and from Dryopis it came finally to the Peloponnesus, where it began to be called Dorian.

## modern-en · semele · 360:0

Among the Greeks, Heracles, Dionysos, and Pan are reckoned the latest-born of the gods; but for the Egyptians, Pan is a very ancient god and is one of those known as the eight gods, while Heracles belongs to the second rank, called the twelve gods, and Dionysos is of the third rank, those born from the twelve gods. Now as to Heracles I have already shown how many years old he is according to the Egyptians themselves, reckoning down to the reign of Amasis; Pan is said to have existed even longer than these, while Dionysos is given the fewest years in comparison to the others, and even for him they count fifteen thousand years down to the reign of Amasis. The Egyptians say they know this for certain, since they have always kept records and written down the years as they passed. Now the Dionysos said to have been born of Semele daughter of Cadmos was born about sixteen hundred years before my time; Heracles, son of Alcmene, about nine hundred years; and the Pan born of Penelope — for the Greeks say Pan was born of her and Hermes — came into being after the Trojan Wars, about eight hundred years before my time.

Cadmus’s daughter, the mother of the Greek Dionysus — sixteen hundred years after the Egyptian one, on Herodotus’s reckoning.

The customs they practise are inherited from their fathers, and they do not acquire others in addition; but besides other customs of theirs worthy of mention, they have one song, that of Linus — the same one who is sung of in Phoenicia, in Cyprus, and elsewhere, having however a name that differs according to the various nations. This song agrees exactly with what the Hellenes sing calling on the name of Linus; so that besides the many other things about which I wonder in matters concerning Egypt, I wonder especially about this — namely, where they got the song of Linus. It is clear, however, that they have sung this song from time immemorial; and in the Egyptian tongue Linus is called Maneros. The Egyptians told me that he was the only son of the man who first became king of Egypt, and that he died before his time and was honoured with these lamentations by the Egyptians; and that this was their first and only song.

Hellenic customs they will by no means follow, and generally speaking they follow those of no other men whatever. This rule is observed by most of the Egyptians; but there is a large city named Chemmis in the Theban district near Neapolis, and in this city there is a temple of Perseus son of Danae, which is square-shaped, and around it grow date-palms. The gateway of the temple is built of stone and of very great size, and at the entrance two great stone statues stand. Within this enclosure is a temple-house, and in it stands an image of Perseus. These people of Chemmis say that Perseus often appears in their land and often within the temple, and that a sandal worn by him is sometimes found, measuring two cubits in length; and whenever this appears, all Egypt prospers. So they say, and they honour Perseus in Hellenic fashion thus: they hold an athletic contest, which includes the whole list of games, and they offer as prizes cattle, cloaks, and skins. When I asked why Perseus appeared to them alone, and why they were separated from all the other Egyptians in holding an athletic contest, they said that Perseus had been born of their city; for Danaus and Lynceus, they said, were men of Chemmis and had sailed to Hellas, and from them they traced a descent down to Perseus. They told me he had come to Egypt for the reason the Hellenes also give — namely to bring the Gorgon's head from Libya — and had then visited them too and recognised all his kinsfolk; and they said he had thoroughly learnt the name of Chemmis before coming to Egypt, since he had heard it from his mother, and that they celebrated an athletic contest for him by his own command.

## modern-en · isis · 256:0

The clean males of the ox kind, then, both full-grown animals and calves, are sacrificed by all the Egyptians; but the females they may not sacrifice, for these are sacred to Isis. The figure of Isis is in the form of a woman with cow's horns, just as the Hellenes represent Io in pictures, and all the Egyptians without exception revere cows far more than any other kind of cattle. For this reason no Egyptian man or woman would kiss a Hellene on the mouth, nor will they use a knife, roasting-spits, or cauldron belonging to a Hellene, nor taste of the flesh even of a clean animal if it has been cut with a Hellene's knife. They bury the cattle of this kind that die in the following manner. The females they throw into the river, but the males they bury, each people in the outskirts of their town, with one of the horns (or sometimes both) protruding to mark the place. When the bodies have rotted away and the appointed time comes, then to each city comes a boat from what is called the island of Prosopitis (this is in the Delta, and the circuit of it measures nine schoeni). On this island of Prosopitis, besides many other cities, stands the one from which the boats come to collect the bones of the oxen; the city's name is Atarbechis, and in it there is a holy temple of Aphrodite. From this city many go out in various directions, some to one city and some to another, and when they have dug up the bones of the oxen they carry them off, and all together they bury them in a single place. In the same way they bury their other cattle when these die, for the same law is laid down concerning them, and these too they abstain from killing.

Whose figure is a woman with cow’s horns, and who is Demeter in Greek. Cows are sacred to her and are never sacrificed.

## modern-en · osiris · 257:0

Now all who have a temple set up to the Theban Zeus, or who belong to the district of Thebes, sacrifice goats and abstain from sheep — for not all Egyptians equally reverence the same gods, except only Isis and Osiris (whom they say is Dionysus); these they all reverence alike. But those who have a temple of Mendes or belong to the Mendesian district abstain from goats and sacrifice sheep. The men of Thebes, and those who after their example abstain from sheep, say this custom was established among them for the following reason. Heracles (they say) had an earnest desire to see Zeus, and Zeus did not desire to be seen by him; at last, when Heracles was insistent in entreaty, Zeus devised this trick — he flayed a ram and held in front of him the head of the ram that he had cut off, and he put on over himself the fleece, and then showed himself to Heracles. Hence the Egyptians make the image of Zeus with the face of a ram; and the Ammonians do so also after their example, being settlers from both the Egyptians and the Ethiopians, and using a language that is a mixture of both tongues. And in my opinion it is from this god that the Ammonians took the name they bear, for the Egyptians call Zeus Amun. The Thebans, then, do not sacrifice rams but hold them sacred for this reason. Yet on one day of the year, at the feast of Zeus, they cut up and flay a single ram in the same manner, and cover the image of Zeus with its skin, and then they bring up to it another image of Heracles. When this is done, all who are in the temple beat themselves in lamentation for the ram, and then they bury it in a sacred tomb.

The reason why they represent him in this form I prefer not to say. One of the eight oldest gods in the Egyptian reckoning, painted and carved with a goat’s face and a he-goat’s legs; the Mendesians will not sacrifice goats because of him.

For just at the time when the Athenians were beginning to be counted among the Hellenes, the Pelasgians became dwellers with them in their land, and from this very circumstance they began to be counted as Hellenes. Whoever has been initiated in the mysteries of the Cabeiri, which the Samothracians perform (having received them from the Pelasgians), that man knows the meaning of my speech. For those very Pelasgians who became dwellers with the Athenians used to dwell before that time in Samothrace, and from them the Samothracians received their mysteries. So then the Athenians were the first of the Hellenes to make the images of Hermes with the phallos, having learnt the practice from the Pelasgians; and the Pelasgians told a sacred story about it, which is set forth in the mysteries in Samothrace.

About Heracles I heard the account that he was one of the twelve gods; but of the other Heracles whom the Hellenes know I was not able to hear anything in any part of Egypt. Moreover, to prove that the Egyptians did not take the name of Heracles from the Hellenes, but rather the Hellenes from the Egyptians — I mean those of the Hellenes who gave the name Heracles to the son of Amphitryon — of this, besides many other proofs, there is chiefly this: that the parents of this Heracles, Amphitryon and Alcmene, were both of Egyptian descent; and also that the Egyptians say they know neither the name of Poseidon nor of the Dioscuri, nor have these been accepted by them as gods among their other gods. Yet if they had received the name of any divinity from the Hellenes, they would naturally have preserved the memory of these most of all — assuming, as I suppose and as my judgment compels me to think, that in those times, as now, some of the Hellenes made voyages and were seafaring folk; so that the Egyptians would have learnt the names of these gods even more than that of Heracles. In fact, however, Heracles is a very ancient Egyptian god; and (as the Egyptians themselves say) it is seventeen thousand years to the beginning of the reign of Amasis from the time when the twelve gods — of whom they count Heracles one — were begotten of the eight gods.

Named with Poseidon among the gods the Egyptians say they have never heard of.

## modern-en · ethiopians · 237:0

The third of the explanations is much the most plausible, but nevertheless the most mistaken of all; for this one has no more truth in it than the others, alleging as it does that the Nile flows from melting snow — whereas in fact it flows out of Libya through the midst of the Ethiopians, and so comes out into Egypt. How then should it flow from snow, when it flows from the hottest parts to those which are cooler? Indeed most of the facts are such as to convince any reasoning man that it is not at all likely it flows from snow. The first and greatest evidence is the winds, which blow hot from these regions; the second is that the land there is always rainless and without frost, whereas after snow has fallen rain must necessarily come within five days, so that if it snowed in those parts rain would fall there; the third evidence is the people dwelling there, who are black in colour because of the burning heat. Moreover, kites and swallows remain there throughout the year and do not leave the land; and cranes, fleeing the cold that sets in over Scythia, come regularly to these parts to winter. If then it snowed ever so little in the land through which the Nile flows and in which it has its rise, none of these things could take place, as necessity compels us to admit.

Egypt’s neighbours up the Nile, whose king Sabacos held Egypt for fifty years, and among whom the Deserters settled beyond Meroe.

Moreover, the answer given by the Oracle of Ammon also bears witness in support of my opinion that Egypt is of the extent I declare it to be in my account; and I heard of this answer after I had already formed my own opinion about Egypt. The people of the cities of Marea and Apis, dwelling in those parts of Egypt that border on Libya, held themselves to be Libyans and not Egyptians, and also being burdened by the rules of religious service — because they wished not to be debarred from the use of cows' flesh — sent to Ammon saying they had nothing in common with the Egyptians, for they dwelt outside the Delta and agreed with them in nothing; and they said they desired that it might be lawful for them to eat everything without distinction. The god, however, did not permit them to do so, but answered that Egypt was that land which the Nile comes over and waters, and that those were Egyptians who, dwelling below the city of Elephantine, drank of that river. Thus it was answered to them by the Oracle about this:

Whose land runs west from Egypt; the Marea and Apis people claimed to be Libyans and not Egyptians so as to eat cow’s flesh, and were told by the oracle that all the Nile waters is Egypt.

The Scythians, however, did not make their invasion by this route, but turned aside from it to take the upper road, which is much longer, keeping Mount Caucasus on their right hand. ## modern-en · nasamonians · 247:0

This much, however, I heard from men of Cyrene, who told me they had gone to the Oracle of Ammon and had come into conversation with Etearchus king of the Ammonians; and it happened that after speaking of other matters they fell to discussing the Nile and how no one knew its sources. Etearchus said that once there had come to him men of the Nasamonians (this is a Libyan people who dwell in the Syrtis, and also in the land east of the Syrtis for no great distance), and when these Nasamonians came, and were asked by him whether they could tell him anything more than he knew about the desert parts of Libya, they said there had been among them certain sons of chief men, who were of unruly disposition; and these, when they grew up to be men, had devised various extravagant undertakings, and among them they drew lots for five of themselves to go and see the desert parts of Libya and try whether they could discover more than those who had previously explored furthest. For in the parts of Libya along the Northern Sea, beginning from Egypt and going as far as the headland of Soloeis (which is the extreme point of Libya), Libyans — and many races of them — extend along the whole coast, except such portions as the Hellenes and Phoenicians hold. But in the upper parts, which lie above the seacoast and above the peoples whose land comes down to the sea, Libya is full of wild beasts; and in the parts beyond the land of wild beasts it is full of sand, terribly waterless, and utterly desert. These young men then (they said), being sent out by their companions well supplied with water and provisions, went first through the inhabited country; after they had passed through this, they came to the country of wild beasts; and after this they passed through the desert, making their way towards the West Wind. Having crossed a great tract of sand in many days, they saw at last trees growing on a level place; and when they came up to them, they began to pluck the fruit that was on the trees. But as they began to pluck it, small men came upon them — of less stature than men of the common size — and these seized them and carried them off. Neither could the Nasamonians understand anything of their speech nor could those who were carrying them off understand the speech of the Nasamonians; and they led them (so it is said) through very great swamps, and after passing through these they came to a city in which all the men were of the same short stature as those who had carried them off, and black of skin. By the city ran a great river, which flowed from the west toward the sunrise, and in it were seen crocodiles.

The Libyan people of the Syrtis whose five adventurous young men crossed the desert and found a city of small dark men by a great river running east.

## modern-en · samothracians · 266:0

These observances, then, and others besides which I shall mention, the Hellenes have adopted from the Egyptians; but in making the images of Hermes with the phallos, as they do, they have learnt not from the Egyptians but from the Pelasgians. The custom was received first by the Athenians of all the Hellenes, and from them by the rest. For just at the time when the Athenians were beginning to be counted among the Hellenes, the Pelasgians became dwellers with them in their land, and from this very circumstance they began to be counted as Hellenes. Whoever has been initiated in the mysteries of the Cabeiri, which the Samothracians perform (having received them from the Pelasgians), that man knows the meaning of my speech. For those very Pelasgians who became dwellers with the Athenians used to dwell before that time in Samothrace, and from them the Samothracians received their mysteries. So then the Athenians were the first of the Hellenes to make the images of Hermes with the phallos, having learnt the practice from the Pelasgians; and the Pelasgians told a sacred story about it, which is set forth in the mysteries in Samothrace.

Who perform the mysteries of the Cabeiri, learned from the Pelasgians who used to live on the island.

## modern-en · eleians · 375:0

While this Psammis was king of Egypt, envoys came to him from the Eleians, who boasted that they arranged the Olympic contest in the most just and honorable manner possible, and thought that not even the Egyptians, the wisest of men, could devise anything else to be added to their rules. When the Eleians came to Egypt and stated what they had come for, the king called together those of the Egyptians reputed to be the wisest, and when the Egyptians had gathered, they heard the Eleians describe everything it was their duty to do concerning the contest. After they had explained everything, they said they had come to learn what else the Egyptians might find to suggest, anything juster than this. The Egyptians, after consulting together, asked the Eleians whether their own citizens took part in the contest, and they answered that it was permitted to anyone who wished, both of their own people and of the other Greeks equally, to compete. Upon this the Egyptians said that in arranging the games this way they had utterly missed the mark of justice; for it could not be otherwise than that they would side with the man of their own state if he were competing, and thus act unfairly to the stranger. If they really wished, as they claimed, to arrange the games justly, and if this was the reason they had come to Egypt, they advised them to arrange the contest so that only foreigners might compete in it, and no Eleian would be allowed to compete. Such was the suggestion made by the Egyptians to the Eleians.

Who came to Psammis boasting that their arrangement of the Olympic games was the justest in the world, and were told to let strangers compete if they meant it.

There are seven classes of Egyptians: one is called that of the priests, another that of the warriors, and the rest are cowherds, swineherds, shopkeepers, interpreters, and boatmen. This is the number of the classes of the Egyptians, and their names are given them from the occupations they follow. Of these, the warriors are called Calasirians and Hermotybians, and they come from the following districts — for all Egypt is divided into districts.

One of the two Egyptian warrior classes, with their own districts and their twelve aruras of tax-free land.

## modern-en · celts · 248:0

Of the account given by Etearchus the Ammonian let this much suffice, except that, as the men of Cyrene told me, he claimed the Nasamonians returned safely home, and that the people to whom they had come were all wizards. Now Etearchus conjectured this river running past the city to be the Nile; and reason compels us to think so. For the Nile flows from Libya and cuts Libya through the middle; and as I conjecture, judging of what is not known by what is evident to the view, it sets out at a distance from its mouth equal to that of the Ister. For the river Ister begins from the country of the Celts and the city of Pyrene, and so runs that it divides Europe through the middle (the Celts are outside the Pillars of Heracles and border upon the Cynesians, who dwell furthest towards the sunset of all peoples in Europe); and the Ister ends, after running through the whole of Europe, by flowing into the Euxine Sea at the place where the Milesians have their settlement of Istria.

Beyond the Pillars of Heracles, at whose country the Ister begins.
