## modern-en · smerdis-son-of-cyrus · 427:0

When Apis had ended his life from the wound, the priests buried him without Cambyses's knowledge. But Cambyses, as the Egyptians say, immediately after this evil deed became completely mad — not having been really in his right senses even before that time. The first of his evil deeds was to put to death his brother Smerdis, who was his brother by the same father and same mother. This brother he had sent away from Egypt to Persia out of envy, because Smerdis alone of all the Persians had been able to draw the bow which the Ichthyophagoi brought from the Ethiopian king, to the extent of about two finger-breadths, while none of the other Persians had proved able to do so. Then when Smerdis had gone off to Persia, Cambyses saw a vision in his sleep of this kind: it seemed to him that a messenger came from Persia and reported that Smerdis, sitting upon the royal throne, had touched the heavens with his head. Fearing therefore lest his brother should kill him and reign in his place, he sent Prexaspes to Persia — the man he trusted most of all the Persians — with orders to kill him. Prexaspes accordingly went up to Susa and killed Smerdis; some say he took him out on a hunt and killed him there, others that he brought him to the Erythraian Sea and drowned him.

Cyrus’s younger son, whom Cambyses had Prexaspes kill in secret after dreaming that he would sit on the throne. Almost nobody in Persia knew he was dead, which is what made the impersonation possible.

## modern-en · smerdis-the-magian · 458:0

Now while Cambyses son of Cyrus was spending a long time in Egypt and had gone out of his right mind, two brothers, Magians, rose up against him. One of them had been left behind by Cambyses as caretaker of his household. This man rose against him, realizing that the death of Smerdis was being kept secret, that only a few of the Persians knew of it, while the greater number believed without doubt that he was still alive. He therefore tried to seize the kingdom, and he formed his plan as follows. He had a brother—the one who, as I said, rose with him against Cambyses—and this man very closely resembled in appearance Smerdis son of Cyrus, whom Cambyses had killed, his own brother. He resembled Smerdis in form, and not only so, but he had the same name, Smerdis. Having persuaded this man that he would manage everything for him, the Magian Patizeithes brought him and seated him on the royal throne. Then he sent heralds to the various provinces, and among them one to the army in Egypt, to proclaim that from now on they were to obey Smerdis son of Cyrus instead of Cambyses.

The brother left in charge of Cambyses’s household, who seized the throne because he knew the real Smerdis was dead and that almost nobody else did. He was his namesake, and he reigned seven months and did his subjects good; the Persians killed him when Otanes found out, through his own daughter, that the man in the bed had no ears.

## modern-en · nitetis · 398:0

It was against this Amasis that Cambyses son of Cyrus was making his expedition, taking with him not only the other peoples over whom he ruled but also Greeks, both Ionians and Aiolians; and the cause of the expedition was as follows. Cambyses sent an envoy to Egypt and asked Amasis to give him his daughter, making the request on the advice of an Egyptian who urged it on Amasis out of a grudge, for the following reason: at the time when Cyrus had sent to Amasis asking for a physician of the eyes, the best in Egypt, Amasis had picked him out of all the physicians in Egypt and torn him away from his wife and children, and handed him over to Persia. Having this grievance, the Egyptian urged Cambyses on by advising him to ask Amasis for his daughter, so that Amasis would either be grieved if he gave her, or if he refused to give her, would offend Cambyses. Amasis, vexed and afraid of the power of the Persians, knew neither how to give nor how to refuse, for he was well aware that Cambyses meant to keep her not as a wife but as a concubine. Reckoning it this way, he did the following: there was a daughter of the former king Apries, very tall and of comely form, the only surviving member of his house, and her name was Nitetis. This girl Amasis adorned with fine clothing and gold, and sent off to Persia as his own daughter. But after a time, when Cambyses greeted her, calling her by the name of her father, the girl said to him: “O king, you do not realize how you have been deceived by Amasis; for he dressed me up in finery and sent me off, giving me to you as his own daughter, whereas in truth I am the daughter of Apries, against whom Amasis rose up along with the Egyptians and murdered him, though he was his lord and master.” These words, and the occasion that produced them, led Cambyses son of Cyrus against Egypt, moved to very great anger.

Apries’s daughter, whom Amasis sent to Cambyses in place of his own — and who told Cambyses whose daughter she really was. That, in the Egyptian telling, is why Persia invaded.

## modern-en · phanes · 401:0

Another thing also contributed to this expedition, which was as follows. There was among the foreign mercenaries of Amasis a man who was by race from Halicarnassos, named Phanes, who was both capable in judgment and valiant in matters of war. This Phanes, having (as one may suppose) some quarrel with Amasis, fled away from Egypt in a ship, desiring to come to speech with Cambyses. Since he was of no small repute among the mercenaries and was very closely acquainted with all the affairs of Egypt, Amasis pursued him and considered it a matter of some importance to capture him. He pursued him by sending after him the most trusted of his eunuchs with a trireme, who captured him in Lykia; but having captured him, he did not bring him back to Egypt, since Phanes got the better of him by a trick — he made his guards drunk and escaped to Persia. So when Cambyses had resolved to march against Egypt and was in difficulty about the march — how he should get safely through the waterless region — this man came to him and, besides informing him about the other affairs of Amasis, also advised him concerning the march, telling him to send to the king of the Arabians and ask him for safe passage through this region.

The Halicarnassian mercenary who quarrelled with Apries, fled to Cambyses, and told the Persians how to cross the waterless desert. The Greeks in Egyptian service cut his sons’ throats in front of him before the battle and drank the blood.

## modern-en · psammenitos · 407:0

Meanwhile, Psammenitos the son of Amasis was camped at the Pelusian mouth of the Nile, waiting for Cambyses. Cambyses did not find Amasis alive when he invaded Egypt. Amasis had died after a forty-four-year reign, during which he had suffered no great misfortune. When he died, he was embalmed and placed in the tomb he had built for himself in the temple. During the reign of his son Psammenitos, the Egyptians witnessed the greatest portent ever seen: it rained in Egyptian Thebes. Rain had never fallen there before that time, and has not fallen since, according to the Thebans themselves. In upper Egypt, rain simply does not fall. Yet on this occasion, it rained in Thebes—a drizzle.

Amasis’s son, king of Egypt for six months, who did not weep to see his daughter carrying water or his son led out to die, and wept at the sight of an old drinking-companion begging. Cambyses spared him and then killed him for plotting.

## modern-en · apis · 233:0

Moreover, the answer given by the Oracle of Ammon also bears witness in support of my opinion that Egypt is of the extent I declare it to be in my account; and I heard of this answer after I had already formed my own opinion about Egypt. The people of the cities of Marea and Apis, dwelling in those parts of Egypt that border on Libya, held themselves to be Libyans and not Egyptians, and also being burdened by the rules of religious service — because they wished not to be debarred from the use of cows' flesh — sent to Ammon saying they had nothing in common with the Egyptians, for they dwelt outside the Delta and agreed with them in nothing; and they said they desired that it might be lawful for them to eat everything without distinction. The god, however, did not permit them to do so, but answered that Egypt was that land which the Nile comes over and waters, and that those were Egyptians who, dwelling below the city of Elephantine, drank of that river. Thus it was answered to them by the Oracle about this:

The sacred calf of Memphis, whom the Greeks call Epaphos: black with a white diamond, a double hair in its tail, the marks of an eagle and a beetle. Cambyses stabbed it in the thigh and it died in the temple, which the Egyptians say is why he went mad.

Such is the nature of the so-called Table of the Sun as described. So when Cambyses had resolved to send the spies, he at once sent for those of the Ichthyophagoi who understood the Ethiopian tongue to come from the city of Elephantine. While they were going to fetch these men, he gave the order for the fleet to sail against Carthage. But the Phoenicians said they would not do so, for they were bound not to by solemn vows, and they would not be acting piously by making an expedition against their own sons. Since the Phoenicians were unwilling, the rest were rendered unequal to the attempt. Thus the Carthaginians escaped being enslaved by the Persians; for Cambyses did not think it right to use force to compel the Phoenicians, both because they had delivered themselves over to the Persians of their own accord and because the entire naval force depended on the Phoenicians. The men of Cyprus too had delivered themselves over to the Persians and were joining in the expedition against Egypt.

The Fish-eaters of Elephantine, who knew the Ethiopian tongue and were sent ahead of Cambyses’s army as spies with gifts.

## modern-en · achaimenes · 409:0

I was witness, moreover, to a great marvel, which the local inhabitants pointed out to me. Of the bones scattered about of those who fell in this fight — each side lying separately, since the bones of the Persians were heaped on one side, where they had been laid apart at the start, and those of the Egyptians on the other — the skulls of the Persians are so weak that if you hit them with just a pebble you will make a hole in them, while those of the Egyptians are so exceedingly strong that you would hardly break them by striking them with a large stone. The cause of this, they said — and I for my part readily believe them — is that the Egyptians from early childhood shave their heads, and the bone is thickened by exposure to the sun. This is also the reason why they do not become bald: for among the Egyptians you see fewer bald-headed men than among any other race. This, then, is why they have strong skulls. The reason the Persians have weak ones is that from infancy they keep their heads delicately in the shade by wearing tiaras, that is, felt caps. So much for that. I saw a similar thing at Papremis in the case of those who were slain along with Achaimenes son of Dareios, by Inaros the Libyan.

Darius’s son, killed with his army in Egypt by Inaros the Libyan — Herodotus looking forward out of his own narrative.

## modern-en · polycrates · 397:0

Amasis also dedicated offerings in Greece. At Cyrene, he gave a gold-plated image of Athena and a painted portrait of himself. At the temple of Athena at Lindos, he gave two stone statues and a remarkable corselet of linen. At the temple of Hera on Samos, he gave two wooden portraits of himself, which were still standing in my time in the great temple, behind the doors. He made these gifts to Samos because of the friendship between himself and Polycrates, son of Aeaces. His gifts to Lindos were not because of any friendship but because the temple of Athena at Lindos was said to have been founded by the daughters of Danaos, who landed there while fleeing the sons of Aegyptus. Those were the offerings of Amasis. He was the first man to conquer Cyprus and compel it to pay tribute.

Tyrant of Samos, who took the island with fifteen men, made himself the first Greek since Minos to aim at ruling the sea, and was crucified at Magnesia by a Persian governor he had never fought. Herodotus will not repeat what was done to his body.

## modern-en · aiakes · 436:0

Now while Cambyses was marching against Egypt, the Lacedemonians had also made an expedition against Samos and against Polycrates son of Aiakes, who had risen up against the government and gained power over Samos. At first he had divided the state into three parts and given a share to his brothers Pantagnotos and Syloson; but afterwards he put one of them to death, and drove out the younger, Syloson, and so obtained possession of all Samos. Then, being in possession, he made a guest-friendship with Amasis king of Egypt, sending him gifts and receiving gifts in return. After this, within a short period, the power of Polycrates increased rapidly, and there was much fame of it not only in Ionia but also over the rest of Hellas: for wherever he directed his forces, everything went fortunately for him. He had gotten for himself a hundred fifty-oared galleys and a thousand archers, and he plundered all men indiscriminately, making no distinction; for he used to say that he would earn more gratitude from a friend by giving back what he had taken than by not taking at all. So he had conquered many of the islands and many cities on the mainland, and besides other things he won a sea-fight against the Lesbians as they were coming with their forces to aid the Milesians, and defeated them; and these men dug the whole trench round the wall of the city of Samos while working in chains.

Polycrates’s father.

## modern-en · maiandrios · 520:0

When Polycrates heard this he rejoiced and was ready to agree; and since, it seems, he had a great desire for wealth, he first sent Maiandrios son of Maiandrios, a native of Samos who was his secretary, to see it. This was the same man who, not long after these events, dedicated to the temple of Hera all the ornaments of the men's chamber in the palace of Polycrates—ornaments well worth seeing. Oroetes, having heard that the man sent to examine the treasure would soon arrive, did as follows: he filled eight chests with stones, except for a small space at the very top of each, and laid gold on top of the stones. Then he tied up the chests and kept them ready. So Maiandrios came, looked at them, and brought word back to Polycrates.

Polycrates’s secretary, left in charge of Samos, who tried to hand the island over to a free constitution and was refused; he ended by opening the treasury to the Persians and escaping through a tunnel.

## modern-en · charilaos · 542:0

Now the despot Maiandrios had a brother who was somewhat mad; his name was Charilaos. For some offence this man had committed, he had been confined in an underground dungeon. At the time of which I speak, having heard what was happening, and putting his head out of the dungeon, he saw the Persians peacefully seated there and began to cry out, saying he wished to speak with Maiandrios. Maiandrios, hearing his voice, ordered him loosed and brought before him. As soon as he was brought, he began to abuse and revile his brother, trying to persuade him to attack the Persians. He said: “You basest of men, you put me in chains and thought me worthy of the underground dungeon—me, your own brother, who had done nothing worthy of bonds. Now you see the Persians casting you out of the land and making you homeless, and you dare take no revenge, though they are so very easy to overcome? But if you are truly afraid of them, give me your mercenaries and I will take vengeance on them for their coming here; and you I am willing to let go out of the island.”

Maiandrios’s half-mad brother, who was let out of the cells, saw the Persians sitting unarmed, took the mercenaries and killed them — and so brought the massacre of Samos on the island.

## modern-en · archias-samos · 452:0

If all the Lacedaemonians present that day had been the equal of Archias and Lycopas, Samos would have been taken. For Archias and Lycopas alone rushed inside the wall together with the fleeing Samians, and being cut off from retreat were killed within the city of Samos. I myself spoke in Pitane—for that was the deme he belonged to—with the third in descent from this Archias: another Archias, son of Samios, son of Archias, who honoured the Samians above all other foreigners. He told me that his own father had been named Samios because Archias, his grandfather, had died a glorious death in Samos. And he said he honoured Samians because his grandfather had been granted a public funeral by them.

One of the two Lacedaemonians who broke into Samos and died inside it; Herodotus met his grandson, also Archias, at Pitana.

## modern-en · eupalinos · 457:0

I have spoken at some length about the Samians because they have three works that are greater than any others ever constructed by Hellenes. First, a tunnel, open at both ends, dug through a mountain no less than one hundred and fifty fathoms high. The tunnel is seven furlongs long and eight feet high and wide, and throughout its whole length another channel has been cut twenty cubits deep and three feet wide, through which water is piped to the city from an abundant spring. The designer of this work was a Megarian, Eupalinos son of Naustrophos. That is one of the three. The second is a mole in the sea around the harbour, reaching down as much as twenty fathoms; its length is more than two furlongs. The third work they have accomplished is a temple larger than any other temple we know of. Its first designer was Rhoicos son of Philes, a native of Samos. For these reasons I have spoken at greater length of the Samians.

The Megarian engineer of the Samian tunnel, seven furlongs through a mountain and open at both ends — the first of the three greatest Greek works Herodotus knows.

The third work they have accomplished is a temple larger than any other temple we know of. Its first designer was Rhoicos son of Philes, a native of Samos. For these reasons I have spoken at greater length of the Samians.

Eupalinos’s father.

## modern-en · oroetes · 517:0

Now about the time of Cambyses' illness, this had come to pass. There was a certain Oroetes, a Persian, whom Cyrus had appointed governor of the province of Sardis. This man had set his heart on an unholy thing. Though he had never suffered any wrong from Polycrates the Samian, nor heard a single offensive word from him, nor even seen him, he desired to seize him and put him to death, for a reason of this kind, as most who tell the story say. While Oroetes and another Persian named Mitrobates, governor of the province of Daskyleion, were sitting at the door of the king's court, they passed from words to a quarrel. As they disputed their respective claims to distinction, Mitrobates taunted Oroetes and said: “Do you count yourself a man? You have never yet won the island of Samos for the king, though it lies close to your province and is so extraordinarily easy to take that a native of it rose up against the government with fifteen men-at-arms, seized the island, and is now its despot.” Some say that because he heard this and was stung by the reproach, he conceived the desire not so much to take vengeance on the man who said it, as to destroy Polycrates at all costs, since it was on account of him that he was spoken of ill.

The Persian governor of Sardis who had Polycrates crucified out of nothing but pique at a slight he had imagined, and murdered Mitrobates and his son as well. Darius had him killed by a letter read out to his own guards.

## modern-en · bagaios · 525:0

Thus Darius asked, and thirty men undertook the task, each eager to do it himself. Darius stopped their contention and bade them cast lots. When they did so, Bagaios son of Artontes drew the lot out of them all. Having drawn the lot, Bagaios acted as follows: he wrote many papers dealing with various matters and set Darius' seal on them, and with these he went to Sardis. When he arrived there and came into Oroetes' presence, he took the covers off the papers one by one and gave them to the Royal Secretary to read—for all provincial governors have Royal Secretaries. Now Bagaios gave the papers in order to test the spearmen of the guard, to see whether they would accept the idea of revolting from Oroetes. When he saw that they paid great reverence to the papers and still greater reverence to the words read from them, he handed over another paper containing these words: “Persians, King Darius forbids you to serve as guards to Oroetes.” Hearing this, they lowered the points of their spears before him. Then Bagaios, seeing that they were obedient to the paper on this point, took courage and gave the last paper to the secretary. In it was written: “King Darius commands the Persians in Sardis to kill Oroetes.” So the spearmen of the guard, when they heard this, drew their swords and killed him on the spot. Thus retribution for the murder of Polycrates the Samian overtook Oroetes.

The son of Artontes who drew the lot, went to Sardis with a bundle of letters, and had Oroetes’s own bodyguard kill him by reading the last one aloud.

## modern-en · melissa · 447:0

Now Periander had chosen out the sons of the leading men of Corcyra and was sending them to Sardis to be made eunuchs in order to take his revenge, since the Corcyreans had first begun the offense and had done him a deed of reckless wrong. For after Periander had killed his wife Melissa, it happened to him to experience another misfortune in addition to what had already happened, and it was this. He had by Melissa two sons, one seventeen and the other eighteen years old. Their mother's father Procles, who was despot of Epidauros, sent for these sons to visit him and entertained them kindly, as might be expected since they were the sons of his own daughter. When he was sending them back, he said in taking leave of them: “Boys, do you know who killed your mother?” The elder of the two took no notice of this saying, but the younger, whose name was Lycophron, was so greatly grieved at hearing it that when he returned to Corinth, he would neither address his father, nor speak to him when his father tried to converse with him, nor give any reply when asked questions, regarding him as the murderer of his mother. At length Periander, enraged with his son, drove him out of his house.

Periander’s wife, whom he killed, and whose ghost would not name the hiding-place of a deposit until he had burnt real clothes for her in the pit at Corinth.

## modern-en · corcyreans · 445:0

The Corinthians also took part zealously in this expedition against Samos, so that it might be carried out; for an offense had been committed against them as well by the Samians, a generation before this expedition and around the same time as the robbery of the bowl. Periander son of Kypselos had dispatched three hundred sons of the leading men of Corcyra to Alyattes at Sardis to be made eunuchs; and when the Corinthians escorting the boys put in at Samos, the Samians, having learned the story and for what purpose the boys were being conducted to Sardis, first instructed the boys to seize the temple of Artemis, and then refused to permit the Corinthians to drag the suppliants away from the temple. And when the Corinthians cut the boys off from supplies of food, the Samians made a festival, which they celebrate in the same manner to this day. For when night came on, as long as the boys were suppliants, they arranged dances of maidens and youths, and in arranging the dances they made it a rule of the festival that sweet cakes of sesame and honey should be carried, so that the Corcyrean boys might snatch them and so have sustenance. This went on for so long that at last the Corinthians who had charge of the boys departed and went away; and the Samians carried the boys back to Corcyra.

Periander’s enemies, who killed his son to keep him out and whose three hundred boys he sent to Sardis to be made eunuchs; the Samians saved them.

## modern-en · siphnians · 454:0

The Samians who had gone on the expedition against Polycrates also sailed away when the Lacedaemonians were about to abandon them, and came to Siphnos. They were short of money, and the Siphnians at that time were at the peak of their prosperity and had more wealth than any of the other islanders, since they had mines of gold and silver on their island. So rich were these mines that there is a treasury dedicated at Delphi from the tithe of their yield, furnished on a par with the wealthiest of those treasuries; and the people used to divide the yearly income from the mines among themselves. So when they were founding the treasury, they consulted the Oracle as to whether their present prosperity would last them a long time, and the Pythian prophetess gave them this reply:

At the height of their wealth from gold and silver mines, who refused the exiled Samians a loan and lost a hundred talents and their fields for it — as their oracle about the white council-house had warned them.

## modern-en · aeginetans · 393:0

Amasis became a friend of the Greeks. Among other tokens of friendship, he granted the city of Naucratis to Greeks who came to Egypt, giving them a place to live. For those who did not wish to settle but came on trading voyages, he set aside areas where they could erect altars and sacred precincts to their gods. The largest, most famous, and most used of these is called the Hellenion. It was founded jointly by the following cities: of the Ionians, Chios, Teos, Phocaea, and Clazomenae; of the Dorians, Rhodes, Cnidos, Halicarnassos, and Phaselis; of the Aeolians, Mytilene alone. The Hellenion belongs to these cities, and they are the ones who appoint the market supervisors. Other cities that claim a share have no right to one. Separately, the Aeginetans established a precinct of Zeus, the Samians one of Hera, and the Milesians one of Apollo.

Who with the Cretans defeated the Samian settlers at Kydonia and dedicated the ships’ boar-prows in the temple of Athena on Aegina.

## modern-en · otanes · 465:0

This proclamation, I say, he made as soon as he established himself on the throne. But in the eighth month his identity was discovered, and it came about in this way. There was a certain Otanes son of Pharnaspes, inferior to no Persian in birth or wealth. This Otanes was the first to suspect that the Magian was not Smerdis son of Cyrus but the person he really was, drawing his inference from these facts: that he never went out of the fortress, and that he did not summon any of the distinguished men among the Persians into his presence. Having formed this suspicion, he proceeded as follows. Cambyses had married Otanes' daughter, whose name was Phaidyme; and this same daughter the Magian was now keeping as his wife, living with her as with all the other wives of Cambyses. So Otanes sent a message to this daughter, asking who the man was beside whom she slept—whether Smerdis son of Cyrus or some other. She sent back word that she did not know, for she had never seen Smerdis son of Cyrus, nor did she know otherwise who the man was who lived with her. Otanes then sent a second time and said: “If you yourself do not know Smerdis son of Cyrus, then ask Atossa who this man is with whom both she and you live as wives. For she must surely know her own brother.”

Son of Pharnaspes, the first to suspect that the man on the throne was not Smerdis, and the one who proved it through his own daughter. He argued for democracy against the other six, withdrew from the contest for the throne on condition that his house be free, and it still was in Herodotus’s day.

## modern-en · intaphrenes · 467:0

Then Otanes took with him Aspathines and Gobryas, who were leading men among the Persians and his own most trusted friends, and told them the whole matter. They, as it then appeared, had suspicions themselves that this was so; and when Otanes laid the matter before them, they readily accepted his proposal. It was resolved among them that each should associate with himself the Persian he most trusted. So Otanes brought in Intaphrenes, Gobryas brought in Megabyzos, and Aspathines brought in Hydarnes. When they had thus become six, Darius son of Hystaspes arrived at Susa, coming from the land of Persia, of which his father was governor. Accordingly, when he came, the six Persians resolved to associate Darius with themselves as well.

One of the seven, whom Otanes brought in; he later forced the palace gates believing the law let him, and Darius destroyed his whole family but for the brother and son his wife chose. The two editions spell him Intaphrenes and Intaphernes.

## modern-en · oibares · 482:0

Now Darius had a clever groom whose name was Oibares. To this man, after they had left their assembly, Darius said: “Oibares, we have resolved about the kingship as follows: that the man whose horse neighs first at sunrise, when we are mounted, shall be king. So if you have any cleverness, contrive that we may win this prize, and no one else.” Oibares replied: “My master, if it truly depends on this whether you be king or not, take heart on this score and keep your courage high, for no other shall be king before you—such are the charms I have at my command.” Then Darius said: “If you have any such trick, now is the time to devise it and not to put it off, for our contest is tomorrow.” Oibares, hearing this, did as follows. As night was falling, he took one of the mares—the one which Darius' horse most preferred—and led her into the suburb of the city and tied her up. Then he brought up Darius' horse, led him around her for some time—bringing him so close that he touched the mare—and finally let the horse mount her.

Darius’s groom, who won him the kingdom before sunrise with a mare, a hand and a horse that neighed first.

## modern-en · parmys · 485:0

So Darius son of Hystaspes had been declared king, and throughout Asia all but the Arabians were his subjects, having been subdued first by Cyrus and then again by Cambyses. The Arabians, however, never submitted to the Persians as subjects, but had become guest-friends when they let Cambyses pass through to Egypt—for without the Arabians' consent, the Persians could not have invaded Egypt. Darius made the most noble marriages possible in the estimation of the Persians. He married two daughters of Cyrus: Atossa and Artystone. Atossa had previously been the wife of Cambyses her brother, and then of the Magian; Artystone was a virgin. Besides them he married the daughter of Smerdis son of Cyrus, whose name was Parmys; and he also took to wife the daughter of Otanes, the man who had exposed the Magian. Everything became filled with his power. And first he had a carving in stone made and set up, showing a man on horseback, and he wrote on it an inscription to this effect: “Darius son of Hystaspes, by the excellence of his horse,”—naming it—“and of his groom Oibares, obtained the kingdom of the Persians.”

Smerdis’s daughter, one of the wives Darius took to fasten the succession to Cyrus’s line.

## modern-en · zopyros · 550:0

Then in the twentieth month a prodigy befell Zopyros, son of that Megabyzos who had been one of the seven men who killed the Magian. To this Zopyros, son of Megabyzos, a prodigy occurred: one of the mules that served as bearers of provisions for him gave birth. When this was reported to him, and Zopyros had himself seen the foal—for he did not believe the report—he charged those who had seen it to tell no one what had happened, and he considered with himself what to do. Bearing in mind the words of the Babylonian who had said at the start that when mules bore young the wall would be taken—remembering, I say, this ominous saying—it seemed to Zopyros that Babylon could be taken. For he thought that both the man had spoken and his mule had foaled by divine dispensation.

Megabyzos’s son, to whom a mule foaled; he cut off his own nose and ears, had himself whipped, deserted to Babylon as a mutilated man with a grievance, was given the city’s army, and handed the walls to Darius. Darius said he would rather have one Zopyros unmutilated than twenty Babylons.

## modern-en · demokedes · 522:0

Polycrates, however, disregarded every counsel and set sail for Oroetes, taking with him, besides many other friends, Demokedes son of Calliphon, a man of Croton who was a physician and practised his art better than anyone else of his time. When he arrived at Magnesia, Polycrates was miserably put to death in a manner unworthy both of himself and of his lofty ambitions. For apart from those who became despots of the Syracusans, none of the other Hellenic despots is worthy to be compared with Polycrates in magnificence. And when Oroetes had killed him in a way not fit to be told, he impaled his body. Of those who had come with him, he released all the Samians, bidding them be grateful to him for being free men; but all those in his company who were either allies or servants, he kept as slaves. Polycrates, then, being hung up, fulfilled his daughter's vision entirely: for he was bathed by Zeus whenever it rained, and anointed by the Sun as he gave off moisture from his body.

The physician of Croton, sold into Persia as Polycrates’s slave, who cured Darius’s ankle and Atossa’s breast, got everything he asked for except his freedom, and talked his way home by way of a reconnaissance of Greece. The two editions spell him Demokedes and Democedes.

## modern-en · milon · 534:0

The Persians, setting sail from there and pursuing Demokedes, reached Croton, and finding him in the market-place, laid hands on him. Some of the men of Croton, fearing Persian power, were willing to let him go; but others took hold of him and struck at the Persians with their staves, while the Persians pleaded: “Men of Croton, take care what you are doing. You are rescuing a man who was a slave of King Darius and ran away from him. How do you think King Darius will be content to receive such an insult? And how can this act be well for you, if you take him away from us? What city, do you think, shall we make an expedition against sooner than this, and what city shall we try to enslave before this?” Saying this, however, they did not persuade the men of Croton. Having had Demokedes rescued from them and the cargo ship they had brought with them seized, they set sail to return to Asia, and did not try to visit or investigate any more parts of Hellas, now that they were deprived of their guide. This, however, Demokedes gave them as a charge when they were putting out to sea: to tell Darius that Demokedes was betrothed to the daughter of Milon. For the wrestler Milon had a great name at the king's court; and I suppose that Demokedes pressed for this marriage, spending a great deal of money to bring it about, so that Darius might see that he was held in honour in his own country as well.

The wrestler of Croton, whose daughter Demokedes bought himself a betrothal to, in name, because Darius had heard of him.

## modern-en · aristophilides · 533:0

So these men, having gone down to Phoenicia and to the city of Sidon there, at once manned two triremes, and besides them loaded a large cargo ship with goods of every kind. Then when they had made everything ready they set sail for Hellas, and touching at various places they viewed the coastal regions and wrote a description, until at last, when they had seen most of the famous places, they arrived at Taras in Italy. There, out of goodwill toward Demokedes, Aristophilides the king of the Tarentines unfastened and removed the steering-oars of the Median ships, and also confined the Persians in prison, alleging they came as spies. While they were being dealt with this way, Demokedes went off and reached Croton. When he was safely in his own native place, Aristophilides set the Persians free and returned to them the parts of their ships he had taken off.

King of the Tarentines, who unshipped the Persian ships’ steering-oars and locked up the crews so that Demokedes could get away to Croton.

## modern-en · gillos · 535:0

The Persians, however, after putting out from Croton, were cast away with their ships in Iapygia. As they were being held there as slaves, Gillos, a Tarentine exile, rescued them and brought them back to King Darius. In return, Darius offered him whatever he desired; and Gillos chose the right to return to Taras, narrating first the story of his misfortune. In order not to disturb all Hellas—as would be the case if on his account a great expedition sailed to invade Italy—he said it was enough for him that the men of Cnidos should be the ones who brought him back, without any others. For he supposed that by their means, since they were friends of the Tarentines, his return from exile could most easily be arranged. Darius accordingly, having promised, carried it out: he sent a message to Cnidos and bade them bring Gillos back to Taras. The men of Cnidos obeyed Darius, but they could not persuade the Tarentines, and were not strong enough to use force. So these things turned out this way. These were the first Persians to come from Asia to Hellas, and for the reason stated, these were sent as spies.

The Tarentine exile who rescued the stranded Persians and brought them back to the king, and asked in payment only to be restored — which the Cnidians could not manage.

## modern-en · indians · 435:0

It is clear to me, therefore, by every kind of proof, that Cambyses was exceedingly mad; for otherwise he would not have undertaken to mock religious rites and customary observances. For if one were to propose to all men a choice, bidding them select the best customs out of all the customs that exist, each race, after examining them all, would choose those of their own people; thus all think their own customs by far the best. And so it is unlikely that anyone but a madman would make jest of such things. That all men are accustomed to think this way about their customs, we may judge by many other proofs and especially by the following. Darius, in the course of his reign, summoned those of the Greeks who were present in his land and asked them for what price they would consent to eat the bodies of their fathers when they died; and they answered that for no price would they do so. After this Darius summoned those Indians who are called Callatians, who eat their parents, and asked them in the presence of the Greeks — who understood what was said by help of an interpreter — for what payment they would consent to burn the bodies of their fathers when they died; and they cried out loud and begged him to keep silence from such words. Thus, then, these things are established by custom, and I think Pindar spoke rightly in his verse when he said that “of all things, law is king.”

The most numerous nation Herodotus knows, whose Callatians eat their dead and who pay their tribute in gold dust.

## modern-en · tribute-nations · 152:0

When the herald had spoken, Cyrus reportedly asked the Greeks in his company who the Spartans were and how many. On hearing the answer, he told the Spartan herald: "I have never yet feared the kind of men who have a special place in the center of their city where they gather to cheat each other with oaths." This was a jab at the Greeks for having marketplaces, something the Persians do not use—they never buy or sell in markets. He added: "If I stay healthy, it will be their own misfortunes they discuss, not those of the Ionians." Cyrus aimed this threat at all the Greeks, since they had marketplaces. He then appointed a Persian named Tabalos as governor of Sardis and gave a Lydian named Pactyas the task of transporting Croesus' gold. Cyrus himself marched back toward Agbatana, taking Croesus with him. He considered the Ionians too unimportant to deal with personally. Babylon, the Bactrians, the Sacae, and the Egyptians—those were his primary concerns. He intended to lead campaigns against them himself and send a subordinate to handle the Ionians.

Darius’s twenty provinces, named in a roll of some forty peoples — Chorasmians, Sogdians, Bactrians, Caspians, Parthians, Sarangians, Paricanians and the rest — each with its assessment in talents. The list is Herodotus’s proof of what the empire actually was: not a conquest story but an accounts book.
